GRAPPENHALL.

PARISH COUNCIL MEETING.—On Monday evening, a special Parish Council meeting was convened in the schoolroom, when there were present, Mr. J. C. Parr (chairman), Messrs. Avery, Bradford, Ellison, Warbur- ton, Rathbone, Jenkins and Smith. A letter was read by the Chairman from the Clerk to the Runcorn Rural District Council on the question of providing evening classes anti asking that the matter might be submitted to the Parish Council for their opinion thereon, and sug- gesting that it might be prudent to ascertain the views of the School Committee on the same question. The Rector (Rev. Richard Greenall) and the Schoolmaster (Mr. Evans) were both present and gave the Council the benefit of their past experience in connection with evening classes, the former suggesting that woodcarving or cookery classes would be both attractive and useful. Mr. Bradford asked if it would not affect the rates, whereupon Mr. Evans said the rates would not be affected in the least, since the Government defrayed the expenses, and the grants were more than enough to cover the outlay. Mr. Jenkins said he was surprised at the statement made by Mr. Evans, and thought he knew better than his statement indicated. Every half-penny of the money would have to come from the rates and whether the classes cost much or little the rates would suffer pro- portionately. As a representative of the ratepayers he thought that some of the farmers who might have enough to do to make both ends meet, would object very strongly to pay for teaching children how to carve in wood. And to expect the poor ratepayer to contri- bute to such things was not right, and he could not be a party to it. The Runcorn District Council and other bodies had written to the central educational authority complaining of the exces- sively high rate for education. At Stockton Heath, at a similar meeting to that, anything but a readiness was shown to adopt the evening classes,and its consideration had been deferred. He hoped that other rural parishes would act in the same spirit and do their best to keep down the ever-growing rates. He moved "that the opening of the evening classes be not entertained."— This was seconded by Mr. Bradford. Mr, Parr expressed sympathy with Mr. Jenkins in the positio-ft which hehad assumed towards the ratepayers, but thought the extra outlay would be very infinitesimal, but would leave the matter with the Council.—Mr. Avery would like to know whether the expenses would actually come from the rates, and was replied to in the affirmative.—The resolution, being submitted to the meeting, was carried unanimously.

SCENES FROM A MAN'S LIFE.

PROVING THAT BILE BEANS CURE THE WORST CASES OF INDIGESTION. This is the "pith" of an interesting account which will impress every reader with the effectiveness of Chas. Forde's Bile Beans as a cure for indigestion and alliect ailments. The full details were given on oath before a Commissioner. -

===== 0002601_19040723_art0009.txt | included=true | included =====
SCENE I.

Thos. Watson, miner, of 10, Victor-street, Cutsyke Castleford, was an unusually strong man and a champion, weight lifter. He began to suffer from indigestion. His strength faded. SCENE 11. In the morning he awakenedwith foul taste iamouth; bad breath. Hands trembled. Didn't want breakfast. At work fell sick, faint, and dizzy. Head ached, and it felt too heavy for his neck. Pains in chest, back,and round waist. Grew weaker and weaker. No food would stop on his stomach. Couldn't workany more. Twelve months went by. Still unable to work. Had got so weak that couldn't walk across kitchen floor without being supported. Gave up hope. Began to take Bile Beans.

SCENE lIL Week by week he improved, and after taking a course of Bile Beans, he was cured of every trace of indiges- tion, was back at work, and was rapidly regaining strength. Cure remaised permanent. Twelve months after being cured, a reporter interviewed him on his cure. "Has your strength been restored?" he asked. For answer Watson picked up two 561 b. weights, and raised them over his head. He thenlifted up one of the weights with one finger, and hoisted it above his head. Moreover, he afterwards held it there until he was photographed. Photograph can be seen. SCENE IV. - -

--- Before a Commissioner for Oaths, Watson made the following sworn statement :-- "1, Thomas Watson, miner, do hereby solemnly and sincerely declare as follows :—That from being a man able to lift up two hundredweight above my head with one arm alone, I was reduced by indigestion and kindred ailments to the weakness of a child, unable to walk without assistance. My health has been fully restored, and my strength renewed, solely through taking Chas. Forde's Bile Beans." To those with indigestion, headache, faintness, pains in back or chest, fulness after eating, wind, constipa- tion, or any liver or stomach disorder, nothing more need be said, except this.—ln purchasing Bile Beans from any chemist, the 2s. 9d. size is most economical. It contains three times Is. I?,d. size. Also don't take substitutes.

PADGATE.

TONTINE CLUB.—In connection with the Padgate Tontine Society a successful main took place the other evening on the green attached to the Padgate Stocks. A social was afterwards held, Mr. J. Willett, of War- rington, presiding. Songs were rendered by Messrs. Greenwood, Fryer, Birtles and several others,after which the prizes were presented to the successful competi- tors. A vote of thanks was passed at the close to the host and hostess for their kind attention. WESLEYAN CHURCH.—The anniversary services in connection with the above church were held on Sunday. The Rev. J. Grange Bennett (Connexional Evangelist) preached in the morning from Isaiah xl. 31, and gave a helpful sermon. The choir rendered the anthem "How lovely is Zion "(G. T. Root), and Mr. G. W. Weaver acted as organist. In the evening the church was crowded. Mr. Bennett's text was taken from Isaiah vi. 5,8, and he delivered a sermon on the effect the "Vision of the Lord" had upon Isaiah. The anthems were ".Come unto Him" (Gounod) and" Rock of Ages." Mr. Brough conducted the singing, and Mr. F. Hickman presided at the organ.

LANCASHIRE HEROES. At its monthly meeting in London on Friday, the Royal Humane Society awarded testimonials to the following persons in Lancashire :—James Moggeridge and Joseph Holt, Woolford, near Bury, for an attempt to save a boy who was drowned in a mill lodgeat Wool- ford ' • Tom Nuttall, grocer. Rochdale, for rescuing a boy from the Rochdale Canal; Thomas Lister, weaver, I3arnoldswick, who, although unable to swim, jumped Into the canal at Salterforth and saved Minnie Brooks ; Joseph Hayes, of Hollinwood, for saving a girl who had fallen into the canal; Robert Lawson Baker, Runcorn, for attempting to save a youth named Johnson Who was bathing in the Ship Canal, on the Bth inst.; Herbert Blair, of Burnley, a private in the 2nd Volunteer Battalion East Lancashire Regiment, for rescuing a private soldier who was being swept away while bathing in the sea at Scarborough; Richard Yates, for saving W. Sumner from death in a disused clay pit at Euxton.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The King and the Workhouse Lad.—The mantle of thoughtful sympathy which adorned Queen Victoria, has evidently fallen upon her son. Many an old soldier who knew that he would never again follow the drums has been cheered, as he lay in the military hospital, by a kind word from the revered late Queen, and we find the same tenderness being displayed by the King on the occasion of his recent visit to Newmarket workhouse. One incident in partimilar has been noted and will long be remembered by the poor folks in that part of Cam- bridgeshire and Suffolk, who are forced by res angusta to seek the hospitality of the Poor Law. A lad was lying in one of the wards suffering from cancer and consumption, and the King—who had probably fdllowed I the example of his royal mother by asking to be told ' of the worst cases—asked him if he could play a mando- line which was near to him. The little fellow, we are told; was too confused to answer, but none know better , than the members of the Royal Family how to set a I diffident person at ease, and His Majesty readily pre- vailed upon him to try what he could do. The boy played the National Anthem' while the King stood bareheaded, and at the close the little musician was delighted to be told that he had played very well. Heiress to Millions.—Few girls in Society have been more sought after than Miss Pauline Astor, whose , engagement to Captain H. Spender-Clay is announced. The present Duke of Manchester was understood at one time to be amongst her devotees, but, for some years past, there was scarcely an eligible party to whom the American Press did not engage the only daughter of Mr. William Waldorf Astor. They have extended the same treatment to Mr. Astor, who, however, goes little into Society, and has been a widowersincelB92. Miss Aston is now in her twenty-fourth year, and was principally chaperoned by Lady Selkirk,but of recent years she has presided over her father's beautiful mansi on at Clieveden, which he purchased from the late Duke of Westminster many years ago. He derives his immense wealth from real estate in New York, but he has long been a British subject, living entirely in this country. There are two sons, but even so, it is expected that Miss Astor will eventually inherit a fortune worth many millions sterling. The Richest Girl in the World.—ln these days, when millions are thought little of, it would be difficult to say who is the richest girl in the worid, but it is believed that Miss Krupp, who, on the death of her father became the chief proprietor of the famous . Krupp Works at Essen, could claim that distinction. The works at Essen alone, employ over 25,000 hands, besides which there are coal and iron-ore mines,steam- ships, and other departments, all of which are con- trolled by a committee of management, but Miss Krupp is said to take a keen interest in the conduct of the I business, and the welfare of the employees, and makes a regular inspection of the works. It would therefore seem that she has inherited some of the capacity for industrial organization, which must have characterised her father, and grandfather, and if so, it will give her an interest and enjoyment in life which are said to be very often wanting amongst the possessors of millions. IWe are all apt to regard as fortunate, the girl who has nothing else to do but play at being a millionaire's ! daughter, but the position no doubt has its drawbacks, and is not altogether to be envied. . Women and Learning.—Fortunately dactors, philoso- phers and critics, have a way of contradicting one !another, and when they tell us we are doing this or that !which is harmful, or in some way outraging the laws of Nature, it is easy to set one attack against another, and cheerfully disregard them all. We may therefore com- fortably leave to others the task of refuting the assertion that the human race is degenerating because girls and women have taken to study, and the higher training, though the suggestion might be sufficiently answered in one word. Yet it was seriously put forward the other day in a paper read before the American Medical Congress. For thousands of years women were denounced for their ignorance and frivolity, but now,it seems,they are equally at fault in seeking the higher paths of culture; however, such contradictions only go to show that in nine times out of ten, common sense is much more precious than the philosophy of the sages. The Visional Powers of Men and Women.—There are some people who appear never to look at you, and yet they can afterwards describe your appearance and details of dress, as correctly as if they had been staring at you all the time. A correspondent in one of the London papers wants to know how this is to be ex- plained, and suggests that women possess this unobserv- able faculty of observation to a much greater extent ;than men. This is probably quite true and might be ' accounted for by the training to which they are , accustomed. A man may stare at anything or anybody, so long as he stops short of actual rudeness, but the same latitude is not allowed to the woman, who has therefore become trained to notice things without appearing to do so. Thus, on meeting a man she wishes to observe, she will probably know more about his appearance than he does about hers even though she may not look at him, as he may de in passing, with . perfect propriety. j Another Colour Craze.—There is a decided fancy at the moment for yellow, trimmed with black. For day ! wear this is an unfortunate contrast, as the effect is ' much too striking for good taste. Those women who , have the courage and complexion to wear a yellow gown ' should keep it all yellow, or trim it with palest green. IThe latter combination however is a dangerous one in the hands of inexperienced modistes, and should only be lentrusted to quite first-rate builders of gowns. An Old World Wrap.—The granny shawl, perfectly square, and folded so that a point comes down the middle of the back, is quite a favourite wrap forsitting out after dinner, either at the sea or on the river. The fabrics employed for these shawls are crepe, silk, cash- mere, and many others suitable for the purpose, and they are almost always finished off with fringe. They are fastened in front, preferably with a big old-fashioned !cameo brooch. Those women, therefore, who possess ! such treasures should search them out,and make use of them while they may. White Muslin and Valenciennes Lace.—There is much enthusiasm among exclusive dressers for the simple white muslin frock, trimmed with Valenciennes lace. Such a toilette sounds inexpensive, but as a matter of fact it is nothing of the kind. There are muslins and muslins, and needless to say the very best only, which runs up to many shillings a yard,is employed. Then the lace must be real, and the amount of work put into such gowns in the way of quaint ruchings, gatherings, and , pleatings, is unprecedented. So the simple muslin frock" represents a total of £lO to £l5, and then there I are its very frequent visits to the cleaners to be reckoned I with. -

Holiday Coats.—Short white pique coats are being worn over any kind of toilette by holiday makers, but especialiy over the dark blue trolleuse skirt, when per- haps it appears at its best. This mode is one which will beappreciated by the girl who finds it impossible to provide a coat to match each skirt, moreover the little pique coat can easily be got up at home, and so save the cleaner's bill. The red serge coat too, with saque back and double breasted front, and ornamented with large mother-of-pearl buttons is also appearing at our holi- day resorts, but this to look well, can only be donned over white or blue skirts.

MAD GE .

SMALLPDX HOSPITAL AS A HOLIDAY HOME' The small-pox hospital recently erected by the Wig- ton Rural District Council in the Cumberland hills is crowded, despite the fact that it was predicted that the building would "never be used." The Wigton Work- house children are spending their summer holidays there —and enjoying themselves too!

FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.'

Voile is a mateiral which ranks very highly in fashionable estimation afid4is of the finest and most transparent description. It is of all colours and is not really very expensive in Itself but, of course, must be made over silk to )rove successful, and this adds to the cost of a voile gown. However, it is one of the most useful toilettes and when trimmed with some pretty lace insertion and plentifully tucked and gauged is very dressy-looking. I saw] a very charming dress of this description, the most delicate shade of pastel green being used over silk of exactly the same shade of colour. It was trimmed by two bands of open silk embroidery through which the silk of the under dress was plainly visible. The bodice had a long yoke extending down the shoulders and across the front and back of the

„ /Nt i 41110 0\ '"r6t4;..z„,x4i /.

===== 0002601_19040723_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
A FASHIONABLE SKIRT.

same silk embroidery, the voile being taste- fully gauged in lines at the lower edge. The style was quite simple, the material being arranged in blouse fashion over the silk lining, from which it was quite separate. Indeed, I find that many girls have 'this year had the silk lining of their best frock made separately from the material and have made the one lining do duty for a muslin frock as well. It is a good plan, and if the lining if kept in thoroughly good repair, the slightest tear being mended at once, and a new frill added whenever there is any sign of the silk cutting, it will wear for the season and serve for both frocks.

The fashionable skirt shown in my first sketch is made according to the smartest of this summer's modes. It is of cream voile over pale pink silk and the full gaugings are skilfully regulated to preserve the outlines of the figure of the wearer down to the knees, where the fulness flows gracefully outward round the feet in soft folds. The lining, which is detached from the voile and mounted on a separate band, is used also to complete a rose pink muslin and lace toilette reserved for warm summer days. This colour of mus- lin is very fashionable and, likewise, extreme- ly becoming and is trimmed with a rather ,coarse make of lace, white valenciennes bcing

A CHARMING HAT.

used to trim white embroidered muslin toilettes. A pink muslin dress completed by a leghorn hat, with a high crown trimmed solely by a wreath of flowers or bands of ribbon is an altogether ideal summer toilette. The hat can be completed if desired by velvet strings, which have a softening effect on any variety of headgear and are becoming to most faces.

In the charming hat illustrated the loose strings are of pale pink tulle tied into a care- less bow under .the chin. The hat is of the variety previously described, with a high crown and is trimmed by a large cluster of pink ;and red roses. It is a very becoming style of headgear and looks equally well seen from the back, where the brim bends down and fits closely to the coiffure. These large cabbage roses are much used on the smartest hats this season and make a trimming at once pretty and becoming, and quite inexpensive. Sometimes some bows of handsome ribbon of a contrasting colour are intermingled with the flowers and a soft faint tone of pale blue looks well interspersed among the pink roses. The muslin sun hat has not altogether disappeared from our midst and a charming sample I saw the other day looked most fascinating crowning the ruddy gold locks of a girl of twenty, the clear white muslin of which it was made being sprinkled over with

A TTJSSORE GOWN.

fine black dots, and the edges of the full frills finished by narrow white valenciennes lace. A wreath of forget-me-nots and their leaves and buds surrounding the crown, it looked altogether becoming and sweetly pretty. The subject of my last illustration gives the most important part of a dainty tussore silk dress. It is of the peculiar fawn shade with which we are all so familiar and is trimmed with string-coloured silk lace. It is simply made but nothing could be more up- to-date for wear during the next two months. The waist is finished by a soft kid belt ornamented with dull .gold buttons. Tussore silk wears extremely well and is delightfully cool, and is, therefore, used for making blouses for morning wear. It has also the advantage of not being easily soiled, which is most important when one is far separated from their favourite laundry and have to trust their belongings to the far from tender mer- cies of strange workers, who often doom them to destruction. This useful silk can be obtained, I believe, in various colours nowa- days, for the usual tone is not one to be worn by a sallow complexioned woman. Spotted muslins look very cool and fresh and with embroidered linen are favoured highly by the best dressed women. They are generally trimmed with coarse lace or embroidery of some kind.

A CONVICT :LOVER.

BY GORDON STABLES, M.D., R.N., Author of "The Cruise of the Snowbird," "From Pole to Pole," "Every Inch a Sailor," "A Millionaire's Grave," "For Honour, Not for Honours," tk.c.

===== 0002601_19040723_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
L -TO ALL APPEARANCZ STARK AND STIFF.

I was but little past my boyhood when I joined the Royal Navy as junior surgeon, and nine years afterwards, when invalided on half-pay, I was still a young man. The half-pay granted by a grateful country to an officer is not enough to make a millionaire of him, and, as I had thoughts of getting married at some4ime. I began to look about for something to do, hot having as yet adopted literature as a profession. I was glad enough to become the provisional partner to a medical man in the Midlands. This gentleman was considerably over seventy, and beginning to tire of the harder work and drudgery of the profession. The largest share of this, therefore, fell upon me. The dis- trict was a wide one, , and, though I rode the bicycle—a gigantic ordittary—no one in those days would have sent twice for a doctor who had once pulled the bell-handle after dismounting from such a machine. So I had to be content with a cob.

But it was during my practice in this district that I first became acquainted with the heroine, and latterly the hero, of this short and "ower true" tale.

There are, in my opinion, two situations in which a beautiful girl shews to the very best advantage. One is at a dance or ball, the other at home, pouring out the tea. I had seen Lily Morris in both, and more than once, and did not know in which most to admire her. She was, indeed, a lovely child, graceful in bearing, winning in all her ways, and as sweet in voice as in face. At this date I could not make a declaration as to the colour of Lily's eyes, but I believe they were blue. They were large, I know, and very dark. I am sure I was a favourite with Lily, just as I was with her widowed father; and I am sure, too, that I had to guard myself in case of falling in love with her. There was considerable disparity of years, for, while she was still in her teens, I was thirty. But this was not all. Lily was an only child and would be wealthy. It is true that her father was not what is all too boastfully called a member of an old county family, for he had made his money in business; but, like all sailor- men, I was modest, and would scarcely have dared to aspire to the hand of an heiress. Well, at all events, if I had ever harboured any such thought, the trouble was spared me when Dick Stanley came upon the scene. He came like a comet, and shone like a comet. I can compare his coming to nothing else. His introduction to the Morrises took place in a curious way, and one certainly not devoid of romance, though this romance explained itself later on. The county of B—, then, is extremely well wooded, and there are many bits of old forests in it, some of them dark and gloomy enough. It was while driving homewards through one of these, rather late one moonlight summer's evening, that the new squire, as Mr. Morris was called, found him- self suddenly attacked by a foot-pad. The only other occupants of the pony-carriage were Lily her- self—and the boy in buttons who sat behind.

The bright round moon certainly never lit up a more villainous countenance than that of this robber man. It was quite of the orthodox burglar type—the huge mouth, the lowering brows, the small, cruel eyes, and the bull-dog jaws. He held a huge revolver pointed straight at Mr. Morris's head.

"Doss up, squire, and be quick and quiet about it. Hain't got over much time to spend over a little job like this. Stop yer bloomin' row," he shouted to the page-boy, who was making the woods resound with his shrieks. "Stop that row, or I'll quieten you with my meat-chopper!" Poor Lily had fainted. Hardly knowing what he was doing, Mr. Morris was just about to part with his gold watch and purse, when the sounds of a horse's hoofs were heard at some distance. The foot-pad lowered his revolver immediately and listened, then, just as a gentleman on a splendid black steed dashed round the corner, he bolted into the wood and disappeared. Not, however, before two shots were fired at him by the new-corner. He rode right up to the little carriage. "Thank God/." he said, raising his hat, "I am in time. I trust the fellow did not succeed in robbing you." "Praise be to Heaven and you, sir, we are safe in every way. My chief concern is now my daughter." Dick, for it was he, sprang from his horse, and threw the reins over his neck. Then he lifted Lily, who had sunk down, in his arms as if she had been a baby, and placed her on a bank of grass and wild-flowers close beside the road. There was no water near, but he fanned her face, and soon she opened her eyes and looked wildly round. "You are safe and sound, young lady," said Dick. "Here, drink a little of this.'

He held a silver flask to her lips as he spoke, and she sipped a little, and was now able to sit up and talk faintly. Her grey-haired father was kneeling by her side, and as she placed her hand in his he fairly broke down, and the tears ran over his face. But he dashed them aside, as if ashamed of his weakness, and earnestly invited Dick Stanley to accompany them home and remain for the night. Now, bearing in mind all that followed, I am convinced that Dick planned this method of intro- duction, the foot-pad being, of course, one hired for the purpose. I was sent for next day to see Lily, and it was then I made this strange adven- turer's acquaintance, for he had • been prevailed upon to spend a whole week at the Grange. I must confess that I have never seen a hand- somer young fellow since. Tall, and dark as to hair and slight moustache, he was altogether gentlemanly and winning. Most calm, too, and self-possessed. I liked him from the 'first, and so did my friend the squire. But there was something else I noticed before three days had passed. Lily Morris liked him, and lam not sure, indeed, it was not love almost at first sight ; if there be any such complaint, and I think there is.

Dick Stanley's stay was prolonged for weeks, the squire never seemine, to tire of him. He was amusing, and the best of company. He was instructive as well, for, though not over five-and- twenty, he appeared to have travelled everywhere, and his adventures in India and Africa with wild beasts and wilder men were thrilling in the extreme. And, moreover, though he had never been an officer, he had lent his sword and the spears of his servants to fiht among the far-off hills of Afghanistan; and sole told or spoke

" --- of most disastrous chances, Of moving accidents by flood and field, Of hair-breadth 'scapes i' the imminent deadly breach."

No wonder that young and innocent Lily Morris listened entranenl to the story of Dick Stanley's life, or that she

"Gave him for his pains a world of sighs

'Twas pitiful, 'twas wondrous pitiful. She wished she had not heard it, yet she wished That Heaven had made her such a man'." Well, I could see the goal to which events were evidently moving. For she appeared to

"--: love hlin for the dangers he had passed,

And he loved her that she did pity them.l' A great part of each evening was spent by Dick and Lily at the piano, he accompanying her on the violin, for he was a skilled performer. And I know nothing more likely to draw two young hearts together than the sympathy of music. I feel sure that when they played together one beautiful melody after another, preluding each with such simple remarks as: "Do you know this piece ? " or, "Oh, have you seen this?" there was no outer world to them; they were a world to each other, a world to themselves.

Dick nd I became very friendly. He treated me e.,s a confidant, and told me of all his love for Lily, with the greatest frankness. "It was a fearfullove," he said, and I believed him, for he looked almost terrible as he spoke. "I shall propose soon," be added, "and if se refuses me--"

He stopped and gave vent to a little forced hugh, which told me plainly that in the case of such an eventuality he would turn his pistOl rgAinst his own heart. Though a physician and a man of the world; I shuddered a little as he laughed, for I could read in his eyes that he was in earnest. But one evening he came rushing into my study without the fcrmality of knocking. Ile was beaming all over as he held out his hand. • . "Conwratulate me ! " h e cried.

I knew what he meant, and did. Then he threw himself languidly into my our chair, and sighed. "What! sighing, Stanley ° "Alas! aye," he answered. "Dear Lily's fathcs is ailing, and she cannot marry me for at least months to come. Poor child' she wept bitterly m my arms as she told me." "But, my dear Dick," I s&id encouragingly, "the time will soon fly past. You will see her every day—for I suppose you will still keep on The Honeys ? " "Yes, I mean to; and probably I 11l corn!: into my property in about nine months' time." The Honeys was a beautiful villa that Dick rented. Surrounded by lovely gardens it was, and half-buried in trees. And here he lived in con- siderable style for a bachelor. He kept two horses, and they were the admiration of everyone who looked at them, especially when Dick rode one. Like everybody else far and near, I took Dick for granted, and everyone believed every word he said and no one thought of inquiring into his ante- cedents. Among the villagers he was known as Captain Stanley, and he gave the shopkeepers and a wine merchant the most literal orders, and never asked the price of anything. And so matters went on for months.

But Dick kept no company at his own house. Seeing that he was a single man, this was hardly expected; and though no party or dinner was con- sidered complete without the mirthful and enter- taining Captain Stanley I believe 1 myself was the only individual who -dropped into his "den," as he called it, to smoke a cigar and drink a glass of wine, when my day's duties seemed over. "Robert," said Stanley one afternoon to his valet, "this is rather a lonely place we live in, and, as I have a considerable amount of money and jewellery lying about, I don't think we are quite safe with- out a dog." "No, sir," said Robert, pleased to be ad- dressed thus confidingly and graciously by his master.

"Well, Robert, a dog-dealer is coming to see me to-night. His name is Walsh. just shew him straight in here when he calls." "Very good, sir." "He'll be here exactly at eight,. so be about and answer the door yourself." „ . . .

"Certainly, sii certainly." Well, Walsh was assuredly no beauty; but your ordinary dog-dealers seldom are. He was closeted quite a long time with Stanley, and then Robert let him out again. But he called another night, and this time brought a dog, a bad and savage-looking boarhound, that Robert was sadly afraid of. Uneasy, they tell me, lies fhe head that wears a crown. Probably; but Ido not believe that even the German Emperor goes to bed with his crown on his head. Be this as it may, the miser who keeps his wealth in gold and jewels at home, even in a fire-resisting safe, must feel uneapy now and then. But just such a fool was old Screechley. He had been a judge in India, and was reputed to be immensely wealthy. Yet he only kept one housekeeper, an inquisitive old dame who knew far more about him and his doings than he had any idea of. Indeed, it was currently reported that she was feathering her nest off the judge, and was not at all particular where she obtained the feathers. Besides her, there was a man, with whom she was very "pack," who worked in the garden, but did not sleep in the house. The house was called The Poplars, and lay in the woods well back from the road, and about a mile and a half from the Grange. One morning early the gardener came for me in a great hurry. Both the judge and his housekeeper, he told me excitedly, had been murdered, and lay dead on the floor. He had found the door open, and it was evident from the single glance he had taken that the safe had been broken into and ransacked.

I sent Thomas to the police-station at once, and without waiting for breakfast mounted my horse and rode off in all haste to The Poplars. I must say that I felt horribly uneasy, for there was blood on the doorsteps, and a pool of dust-covered blood in the passage as well. And not far away lay the white-haired old judge, to all appearance stark and stiff.

11.-A LOST LOVE-A BLIGHTED LIFE.

"Who," I said to myself, as I stepped gingerly over the threshold, avoiding the ugly dust-covered pool of dark blood, "who has done this dastardly deed ? "

I bent over the judge. -Re was gagged„'ut not dead, for he moaned ..nd looked at me most appealingly. It took considerable force to remove that gag. Then, warning him not to speak, I lifted and carried him to his bed, and, applying restoratives and giving a hypodermic injection, I soon had him fast asleep. The housekeeper, too, was found by me, gagged in another room, and received similar treatment. The policeman *soon arrived, and after sending Thomas to procure a village nurse I prepared to leave for a time. Before doing so, I was apprised of the arrival of a magistrate to take depositions. I shewed my authority now, however, and he had to leave and return in the evening. But by this time my patients were not only out of danger, but able to talk. I gave both of them more brandy, and the shock they had received had no bad results.• _ _

Old Screechley was terribly disconsolate at first, but the sergeant of police assured him that he had already found a clue, and doubted not all his wealth would soon be restored to him.

Then he told the judge his story, of hearing a noise in the next room at midnight; of lighting a candle, and, with pistol in hand, going quietly, fearfully, in; of finding two masked men at the already open safe, with dark lanterns; of firing point-blank at them; of being knocked down immediately after and gagged; then remembering no more. From the blood I had found all about, it was evident enough that one of the burglars had been wounded by the judge's fire, for he himself was intact. When the sergeant of police spoke of having found a clue, I took little heed, thinking he merely said so to quieten and calm the old judge's mind, and it was not until next day that I thought anything more about it. The news spread like wildfire, and that evening I went to see my friend Stanley, to give him my own version of the daring burglary. I found him reclining on the sofa—a most unusual thing with him. But he got up to shake hands with me, and then I noticed that he was not only pale, but that he limped. "I had a fall in the garden," he said, "and wrenched my ankle. No, no; sit down and talk • it is not worth your attention. Tell me all about this housebreaking business." I did so, as I smoked a fragrant cigar, telling him at the finish that the sergeant had got a clue. He laughed lightly as he sipped his wine. "Those fellows always have," he said. I thought nothing more of the policeman's clue for several days. Meanwhile, Stanley had visited the Grange in a carriage, being still too lame to ride. He was going to London for a week on business, he told Mr. Morris.

"And right sorry," said Morris, "shall I be to lose you. The evenings are dark and long now, and mind, Christmas will soon be here; so pray do not stay long away, for we shall all miss you." The parting between poor Lily and Dick was affecting in the extreme—so Dick himself told me, for I accompanied him that night to the station, two servants coming in a village cart with his luggage, which was far from light. "I feel strangely sad to-night," he said to me , "indeed, I feel as if walking in the Valley and in the Shadow of. Death. 0 poor Lily! How she wept! I have never seen such grief before. Doctor, you and I have been friends a long time now. Go often to see her, and try to comfort her. And listen: I am a careless, happy-go-lucky kind of a fellow, and certainly no man of business. Well, bills have been accumulating, and although I have not bothered about them, the tradespeople may now be uneasy. Tell any of 'them you happen to see that I am perfectly safe, and on my return will settle all." I promised, and we parted. Day after day passed, and neither Lily nor I had any letter. So great was the shock to the poor girl that she took to her bed, and I had her a 3 a patient. On the morning after this, I met the sergeant. "No, not much progress yet, sir," he said, "but here is the clue—found beside the safe." A gold sleeve-link of strange formation, studded with emeralds and rubies! I staggered as if shot. "Good God!" I cried, hardly knowing what I said—"the :ink belonas to Captain Stanley!" The sergeant laughed and nodded. "I suspected something of the sort," he said, "and the man who visited him was no dog-dealer, but a burglar. That jewel will be the link in the chain of evidence that will send your clever friend to prison, and probably to penal servitude." confess I was terribly shocked. I could 555 it

all now, or thought I could. Stanley sand be s mere adventurer. He had thought to marry Lily at once, and with her to inherit a fortune. But the marriage deferred, with debts and poverty staring him in the face, and with a love for Lily Ott was greater than that for life itself, he had been tempted, and—fallen.

Mine was something more than mere sorrow, it was grief as deep as I had endured for many a day; for, sinner though he was, there was good in the man, and he had been sorely tempted. Punish- ment °pick and terrible must follow, and I could not harve desired it otherwise, for even we medical men, Who believe that every species of crime is but the outcome of disease innate, admit that it is right the public should be protected. But if I was sorry for Dick Stanley, still more was I grieved for Lily. How welkad she bear it when she knew all? A lost love! A blighted life! Well, I am, and always have been, a student of human nature, and would not, at that time, believe I could err in my diagnosis of anything pertaining to the mind. If the crime were brought home tc Stanley, as it must be, the shock would be a fear- ful one for Lily, but continue to lore a convict she could not. I should send her, and her father too, abroad to Italy, to Egypt; and among new scenes she would form new ties, and in the common nature of things she would slowly forget, and be once more happy, though, perhaps, never the same as before. As for Stanley himself, he had played his game and lost, and,. although he might love Lily to his dying day, he would recognise the fact that he could never be to her as he had once been.

A student of nature, however, is not necessarily a prophet, and I could not even have guessed how things were to turn out.. A week or fortnight passed away, and still no letter from Dick. Did he know, I wondered, that the police were on his track, and would seize, at the post-office, letters in his handwriting? I could not tell ; but greatly surprised indeed was I when, one dark evening near Christmas-time, Dick presented himself at my study. He was pale and very haggard. "All is lost," he said ; "the damning evidence of my guilt is in the hands of the police. I have been in hiding, but have made every reparation in my power, and have sent the plunder, gold and jewels and all, to Scotland Yard. But oh, doctor, I must see Lily!" I tried to dissuade him. _ _

"I must!. I must ! I must!" he almost shrieked in anguish. "I must see my love and confess all. I must see her once, just once, before—" He paused. "Before what, Dick ? " There was a strange look in his eyes, that I could easily interpret, "Before—ldie."

He was so terribly in earnest that I determined te arrange an interview between Lily and her lost lover. But it should take place in my own study. And so it did. I was there a portion of the time, and never, never shall I forget that poor girl's anguish and tears. I heard him confess his crime without a word of extenuation or reserva- tion. I heard him tell her she was free, although he would bear her loved image and loving words even to the dark portals of death itself. I heard her answer as he held her weeping in his arms that she never could forget him, come what may, and that even the knowledge of his crime was far more easy to bear than the grief she had already gone through in thinking lie had been false to her, or had ceased to love her. Then I left them for a while. "I will return in an hour, Stanley," I said, before I closed the door. For I. believed I had a duty to perform. I knew he was armed, and that soon after parting with Lily he meant to die by his own hand. I would save his life. Anything rather than suicide. It ill true I might be too late, only I meant to try. Mounting my horse, I rode as I had seldom, if ever, ridden before, and in ten minutes' time was in the police-office. Yes, the sergeant and an assistant would come at once. Within the hour I was back. Then there was the sad parting., and I almost carried Lily to her carriage, where her maid was waiting. The last sound of the wheels had scarcely died away when I met Stanley . hurrying out into the grounds, his hand in his pistol-pocket. But the deed was never done, for almost immediately he found himself pinioned, a prisoner in the hands of the police.

I was at the trial myself some weeks after this. Stanley confessed all, yet 'nothing could wring from him the name of his accomplice. He had the best of solicitors, and never before had I listened to a more.beautifully eloquent, though sad, sad speech. I know the judge was affected, and among the women in the court hardly was there a dry eye. But justice had to be done, and considering everything, the sentence was a lenient one—two years' imprisonment with hard labour. I shook the convict by the hand, andbade him be of good cheer and make his peace with God. Then I hurried away.

thought it was better that Lily should know all, and from me; and she did. But the bitter- mess of her grief seemed past. A week after this she and her father started for the Riviera, and, throwing up my situation, I accompanied them as a friend and medical attendant. Even after a whole year and a half, however, I looked in vain for symptoms of amelioration in Lily's case. I am sure her thoughts by day and her dreams by night were ever, ever of her convict lover. More months passed by, the case was just the same, for who on earth can fathom the depths of true love in a woman 's breast?

Well, I had still another duty to perform, so one eay I saw Lilly's father alone. "Morris," I said, bluntly, "it is my painful duty to tell you that you must choose 'twixt Lily's marriage with Stanley when released, or her eariy death. •

Morris raised his right arm. "By the Heavens above," he cried, "she shall marry Stanley, were he ten times the convict that he is." So, much to my relief and Lily's joy, it was all settled; and some time after this I saw the convict himself, and told him everything. The unfortunate man wept with joy as he wrung niv hand and thanked rue. They were married two months afterwards at Nice. Squire Morris had already sold his estate in B—, and together we all sailed for New York, where the young people—aye, and the father also —were to begin a new life. But I have never seen a more happy couple than Lily and D'ick when, at long, long last. I said "Farewell," and left thew. .

EVENTS OF THE WEEK.

SHOOTING AT BISLEY.

The meeting at Bisley, which has caught 'on so of late years, has more than ever proved itself an enormous success. Not only have the entries this year been splendidly subscribed to in point of numbers but the quality of the shooting has been so excel- lent: and, to crown all, the weather has been simply glorious. The camp is quite imperial in character, and the Colonies are

Widely represented fn all the contests. The Mother Country, however, held her own in the Elcho, which is one of the principal events of the meeting, which is highly Pleasing to the patriotic. Two events which always create a great interest are the diumphrey Cup and the Ashburton Shield.

The former is the annual contest betweett the University Volunteers of Oxford and .cambridge, the shooting battle of the Blues. Ihis year Cambridge won by Bot to 784, Private Mander for the winners making top score with 208. The latter is the Public School Cadet Corps' contest and is always eagerly followed, especially by the lady visitors to the meeting. This year Winchester Avon the shield witn a score of 476, Clifton College being second,l only one point behind,' Charterhouse was third with 467, and Wel- lington and Bradfield, the two chief army schools, bracketed fourth with one point less. The minor contests at! Bisley are legion: but of all the greatest is the King's Prize which !las yet to be shot for. The winner of that IS the hero of the camp and takes first place amongst British marksmen.

RE KING'S NIECE SUFFERING FROM APPENDICITIS

r It was announced last week that Princes Victoria, of Schleswig-Holstein, the eldest daughter of Prince and Princess Christian, bad been operated on for appendicitis at the Prince Christian Victor 'Memorial Home at Windsor. The home is a fine building,

replete with every comfort and all the latest a ppl ances, and is equipped with a splendid nurs- ing staff. It Was by Prin- cess Victoria's "express wish that she was taken there for the operation. The Home was ,erected in mem- ory of her brother, Prince Christian Vi c- tor, who was one of the vic- tims of the oer war, and PRINCESS VICTORIA OF n seems strange SCHLESWIG-HOLSTEI N. that his own f.iisfer should have bean amongst its earliest Inmates. DEATH OF A FAMOUS PRIMA DONNA.

The death of an old London favourite in -Miss Girulia Warwick took place in town last 'week. The famous singer, who belongs to the older generation rather than the present, Passed away at her house in Rathbone Place iZt the early age of forty-seven from failure of the heart. Her own name was JuliaOhren- berg, and she was sister of Arthur Ohren- berg, the well- known concert singer, who died in 1896. Miss Giulia Warwick made her debut as far back as 8 7 2, but strangely enough it was not in the ca- pacity of a vocalist, but in that of a pianist,that she first appeared. However, it was

PRESIDENT KRUGER.

At-one time ex-President Kruger, the grim, rugged 'Ooni Paul of the Transvaal, was the 13utt of public opinion in this country and the most hated man by Britons all over the vv.orld, but time has assuaged the intense .bitterness of feeling which prevailed, and now .111 his death the country is ready to see the points of an undoubtedly great man a patriot, though to our despite. A Tangible mark of public forgiveness may be 'ad into the fact that consent has been wen ta the burial of his body in the ransvaal, and his sentenc-.• of exile has been .ta.nceiled in his death. Oom Paul was too /1141 a factor in politics to be a desirable

resident in the Transvaal after the war, but now that ill-feeling has subsided it would be ungenerous to refuse him burial in the land he practically made. Ex-President Kruger passed away last Thursday at Clarens near Vevey, in Switzerland. His death was principally the result of senile decay, but it

i .. , ... , .__, ...- • -,........., . ~...„-;":. , . . . . ?iish'i --",-,., .., r, 1 (k.r.„• sy— / /

===== 0002601_19040723_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
EX-PRESIDENT KRUGER.

hastened by an attack of pneumonia, which supervened on Saturday week. Paul Nruger was born at Rustenberg in 1825 and trekked from Cape Colony in 1836, being thus from boyhood one of the pioneers of Orange River Colony and the Transvaal. As a young man he was noted as the fleetest and strongest man of his day, the best shot and the most fervent preacher, and in the field and in the council-chamber he soon rose to the top. It was he who was originator of the Majuba Hill campaign, and he was elected head of the provisional Government in 1881. Two years later he was elected President of the Transvaal, which post he held till Lord Roberts entered Pretoria. It is generally admitted that he acted with considerable restraint in 1896 at the time of the Jameson Raid, but on October iith, 1899, he allowed himself to be carried away by his feelings, when he sent the now famous ultimatum. Just over a year later he left the land he had ruled so long, a broken man, and since then he has been a wanderer. Now he is to find his last resting place once lore back in his old country beside the wife he had to leave behind him.

DEATH OF SIR REGINALD PALGRAVE.

Avilrinteresting figure of Parliamentary life has pfissed away in the person of Sir Reginald F. D. Palgrave, K.C.8., formerly Chief Clerk of the House of Commons. Sir Reginald, v.ho had been residing at Salisbury since his re irement in 1900, had enjoyed good health until quite re- cently. The de- ceased gentle- man was created K.C.B. in 1392, and was born at 'Westminster in June, 1829, and the son of Sir FrancisPalgrave K.H., Deputy Keeper of the Rolls. His death has created sot- row among the many friends he made during his long career as an official of the House of Com- mons. He en- THE LATE SIR REGINALD tered the Corn PALGRAVE. mittee Office of the House as

far back as 1853 and subsequently mounted every step of the orf cial ladder till he reached the highest poition as Clerk of the House. The position is one of great responsibility as well as of dignity. The Chief Clerk has to advise the Speaker and the Chairman of Committees on the many knotty points of order that are raised from time to time. He must, therefore, be thoroughly familiar, not only with the rules, but with the traditions of the House. These qualifications Sir Re inald Palgrave undoubtedly possessed. He did not achieve the exceptional fame of Sir ErAdne May, but he was an admirable successor to the great authority on Parlia- mentary procedure. Though essentially a student by nature, his unfailing affability made him popular with all classes of persons with whom he was brought in contact. The thorny and crooked path of Parliamentary procedure is made easier for both old and young members through . Sir Reginald's standard works of reference, "Chairman's Handbook," "House of Commons," and his revised edition of "May's Parliamentary Practice." He was 75 years of age.

THE JAP'S ARMY MEDICAL CORPS.

After a •tour of the world Sir Frederick Treves, the King's surgeon, has just re- turned to England. During his travels ,Sir Frederick spent some months in Japan, where he was afforded every facility for observing the army medical organisation. He was struck with the ingenuity of the army medi- cal field equipment which the Japanese have provided for the war. They are the first nation to realise that the medical equipment for a war is not intended for posterity, that it will be obsolete when the war is over, and conse- quently should not be on the lines of the equipment used in the Crimea war, when every- thing was made to last. They have taken the best they could find in England and Germany, and have improved on it. They are not originators, but give them something good and they will produce something better. That is what they have done in the case of medical equipment. At every turn; Sir Frederick states, the neatness and ingenuity of the material was conspicuous—the light, cleverly constructed stretchers, the way they pack their stores, and the completeness of the arrangements. They pack their parcels bettei than any nation on the earth. According tc Sir Frederick th- have no women in flu

A SAD LOOKING man went into a drug store. "Can you, give me," he asked, "something that will drive from my mind the thoughts of sorrow and bitter recollections?" The druggist nodded, and put up a little •dose of quinine, and worm- wood, and rhubarb, and Epsom salts and a dash of castor-oil, and gave it to him; and for six months the man couldn't think of anything in the world except new schemes for getting the taste out of his mouth.

SIR FREDERICK TREVES.

field hospitals. All the attendants are trained men. As soon as wax: began they emptied all the military hospitals in Japan of their men nurses, who went to the front, and trained women took their places. Their surgery is admirable. I saw the Russians who were wounded at Chemulpho and treated by the Japanese surgeons. They have large universities in Japan, and many of their men have been trained in Germany or in the "United States. The Red Cross Society is a marvellous organisation. It has branches all over Japan. It looks after the soldiers on their way to the front, and when the men were going by train to the ports it posted up notices at all level crossings along the rail- ways so that the villagers would know when the trains were due, and would thus be able to give the • men R. good "send off" as they went by. He (Sir Frederick) congratulated the War Office in having 'sent out to Japan a high officer in the Army Medical Service to study the Japanese methods. It was the first time this course has been followed.

131 68 111 87 Majority for St. Helens, I.

Mr. A. H. CROSETELD had to leave before the close of the match to present the prizes at the fire brigade tourna- ment. Before takinghis departure he thanked the mem- bers of the association for their Kindness and courtesy in inviting him to be present. He was very glad that they had had such a lovely afternoon for the final. He did not know a pleasanter or a better way of spending such a fine afternoon than by playing bowls. Ever since he took a great interest in the game—and it was going back a long time—he had tried to help it on, and he intended to do so as much ashe could intim future. (Ap- plause.) He had never advocated bowls or billiards or any other game as an end in itself, but always as a means to an end. He hoped the game would so be treated and that it would continue to flourish, and he trusted also that the handicap competition which was due to com- mence on Saturday week would be as great a success as it was last year. If a few little prizes would help it on he expected Mr. Willett would remind him of it. (Laughter and applause.) The match over, Mr. J. Willett, chairman of the corn- mittee,lin asking the President to present the cup 4 remarked that the manner in which the competition had" been conducted throughout had' afforded the officials the greatest possible pleasure. Some time ago it was said that bowling was a rather low game but he thought they had taught the bowling world and those who were antagonistic to thorn that bowlers knew how to conduct themselves when they got on a public green. That afternoon he believed every man had done his lever best to win, and that the victors would give their hand to the vanquished and wish them successs next time. He was pleased to see so many representatives of the various works present. Their President, Mr. Samuel Taylor and a host of other gentlemen were always willing to help in anything that would benefit the working man, and so long as they had their support sport was bound to prosper. They ought to feel proud of such men. (Ap- plause.)

Captain CROSF/ELD in handing the cup to Mr. George) Ball, said he had enjoyed watching the match that after- noon. He thought bowls was a very good game, because they could go on playing it as long as they could walk. They could not keep young in body, but they could keep young in spirit, and a game like bowls enabled them to feel they were young even when they got old. His (Captain-Crosfleld's) old master at school (Mr. Bowen) —than whom there never was a finer man—played foot- ball until he was 65—the day of his death. He used to play with the lads in all weathers and it made a very great impression on them. He (Captain Crosfleld) thought very great credit was clue to their Secretary (Mr. Scarisbrick) and the committee for arranging the competition. (Hear, hear.) It was a versihealthy thing for works to meet like that in friendly rivalry. They wanted to get them into touch: He hoped the two teams would accept his invitation to tea, along with the officials. (Applause.) Mr. BALL, in preceiving the cup, said that last year 1" P. and K." were fortunate enough to compete in the final, but they were beaten. He then ',promised that they would try and go one better this season, and they had managed it. (Applause.) He congratulated the losing team upon the gentlemanly game they had played. They had his sympathy, and he hoped they would be successful next year. (Applause.) Captain CROSFIELD then presented each member of

Kidney Disease, when it reaches the point of causing sudden collapse, as in the case of Mr. Mark Young, I landlord of the Victoria Hotel, Victoria-street, South- wick, Sunderland, is nothing more nor less than poison- ing of the brood. The overworked Kidneys fail to clear the blood, and the blood becomes impure. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People have the power of clearing out the poison by making new blood. Mr. Young's case is a good example. "I used to suffer from disease of the Kidneys," he stated, "and I was afraid I should never get well again. Shivering fits were among the symptoms. These used to come on so suddenly and unexpectedly that I was often , attacked when out, and would have to be taken + home in a cab, and put to bed in hot ' blankets to keep me warm. I suffered terribly at times in a way which all those who have suffered from their Kidneys will understand. I was nearly doubled , up with the pain. I slept badly every night, and had to exercise great care in my food. My illness was not aggravated by beer or spirits, as you might think, for although a publican, I am a trict teetotaller. I had been under the care of a doctor for a long time, when I happened to read in a newspaper of a cure by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People; and I immediately decided to try whether they would have any effect on me. They have undoubtedly cured me. "Before Ihad been taking them' for a month I felt a great deal better. My appetite had improved, the pains had disappeared, and in other respects I was quite a changed man. I persevered with the pills, and in a very ___ short time I was quite well again. the winning team with a handsome gold medal, and each member of the losing team with a gold-centred silver medal. He congratulated Mr. Nionks and his team on the very good fight they had made. Alr. S. TIYLOa said it gave him very great pleasure, as it did last year, o know that the players had conducted themselves as bowlers and gentlemen. (Hear, hear.) He was glad to see working men Come together under such conditions, and he honestly believed that with perseverance and good attention on the part. of the officials, the competition could be made into a very good thing. If they continued to conduct them- selves in the manner they had done they could not help but get the sop7ort of the various works in \\Torrington. It was very kind of Captain Crosliald and his brother to have given their support. They knew thoroughly well what patrons they were to true and good sport. (Applause.) Mr. E. IiNOWLFN, a well-known howling expert said the Warrington Works Competition was a thing that ought to be copied by every town in England. Em- ployers of labour should encourage their workpeople to come out in the open and play a game in which there was plenty of enjoyment and a good deal of skill. Sir John Brunner had made four or five greens in the Wirmington district with the specific object of getting his people out in the open air. It was the practice of the firm when any of their workpeople went under the care of the doctor to pay the bills. Sir John's idea was to get the men in the fresh air so as to have no doctor's bills to pay. By encouraging bowling they were doing in Warrington one of the best things they could for the workingmen. A great deal was talked about temper- ance. If they wanted to get a man temperate and healthy get him out in the fresh air and let hint have a glass of beer over a game of bowls. Without labouring- the point he congratulated them upon the fine gather- ing that afternoon, and the successful ending of their second competition. (Applause.) I\lr. GEORGE WA.LKER, of the Cheshire Lines, said the spirit displayed by both teams was very creditable. His pleasure had been tempered with one little regret, and that. was that the Cheshire Lines had not been able to carry away the cup. They would try and follow Mr. Ball's advice and "go one better " next year. The Pearson and Knowles teq,in had their congratulations. (Applause.) On the proposition of Mr. WILLETT, seconded by Mr. GEORGE BALL, a vote of thanks was accorded to the President.

Captain CROSFIELD, responding, said he thought the separation of the classes—the rich fromithe poor—which was very marked in England, and wits perhaps becoming more so, was a dreadful thing. That was one of the reasons why he was so very keen on the National Ser- vice League, because it would draw the classes together. The rich man would have to serve alongside his poorer brother in the ranks, unless he could show he was fit to be an officer. He was in favour of a national democratic militia on the Swiss lines, which would du everybody all the good in the world, and would help to secure the greatest happiness of the greatest number. That was surely what thoy were aiming at. (Applause). Mr. KNOWLES proposed a vote of thanks to the able Secretary, Mr. F. Scarisbrick. No organimation, he said, could succeed unless they had a good secretary. He was the bed rock of the whole association, and from his (Mr. Knowles's) experience of Mr. Scarisbrick he was sure they could not find a batter man to undertake the duties. He also spoke highly of the services of the officials generally. The motion was heartily passed. The players and officials adjourned to the hotel and partook of tea, after which a number of toasts were honoured and a few congratulatory speeches made. Sow's were rendered by Messrs. George Ball, George Higham, J. Gibbons, A. Boardraan, W. Walker, and Hulse, jun.

Mr. AUSTEN CHAit- THE SUMMER BERMAN'S studies of MANCKUVRES. his new Tobacco Duty have induced him to offer a rebate to the importers of stripped leaf, threatened with serious loss by tile Budget. The rebate of three halfpence in the pound on their accumulated stocks is expected to reconcile them to the situation, and the Chancellor of the Exchequer is cheerfully prepared to forego some- thing like a quarter of a million of revenue. It is the way out of the mess he ought to have expected to take. But, as Mr. HAROLD Cox points out in the "Westminster Gazette," it is no answer to the outcry of the critics who complain that the protective duty on tobacco is largely increased in ' spite of Mr. BALrouß's pledge that no protective legislation should be submitted to the present Parliament. When that pledge was given the Prime Minister could have known nothing about tobacco duties, and when Mr. AUSTEN CHAMBER- LAIN decided to increase them he - did not trouble his head about the pledge. This is but a fresh instance of the wholly casual relations of this ' Governwent to the issue raised by the joint pro- gramme of Sheffield and Birmingham. Mr. BALFOUR replied in a tone of gentle pain to Sir HENRY CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN'S demand for a day to discuss the vote of censure. He could not understand why people should be so unreason- able as to suppose that anything his colleagues said or did in the present matter must have some bsaring on the moral credit of the Government. There must be a debate of course, but it would be very inconvenient to the transaction of public business at all. He puts it down to the perverse pique with which the Opposition has all along objected to the series of manceuvres by which he and Mr. CHAMBERLAIN have carried the bulk of their party over to Protection. There are still some Unionists who cannot believe that this is the object of Mr. BALFOUR'S policy, and the vote of censure will be moved as one more effort to I enlighten them. But, if they want a clear admis- sion of Mr. BALFOUR'S intentions, they can find it in his journals, which no longer pretend that there is 1 y difference between his sins and Mr. CH4SIBERLAINS. Mr. BALFOUR could not say wh i the discussion of Mr. ARNOLD FOSTER'S pro- posals would be resumed, and it is improbable that they will be heard of again this Session. They represent one of Mr. BALFOUR'S predica- , rnents, and, for the present he is well out of it. Why talk about the Army any more until next year? Why talk about anything ? If there must be any talk, let it be as brief as the closure, or threats of the closure, can make it.

On the appropriation bill there will have to " some full and comprehensive speaking, and gr; BRODRICK is already trying to get Tibet out Of the running of it by announcing that the Britia.b force will be withdrawn as soon as its minion 1° accomplished. The Russian Government has.beea made acquainted with this purpose, and probablY relishes the intimation as !quite in the frontier views of its awn diplomacy in the Far East. expedition which has to fight every step is alwaYs to be withdrawn when its purpose is aecoar plished. That is the etiquette. But at Prr,, sent Colonel YOUNCHUSBAND is on his way "- Lhasa, and how long he may stay there nobodi can have the slightest idea, the Governmens least of all. Mr. BRODRICK says we will n°V annex Tibet, nor establish a Protectorate, Or seek to control the internal affairs of the count' in any way ; but it is manifest that no satisfactorit arrangement can be extorted from the Gra° Lama unless we have control of the internal affair° of Tibet. To keep any such tarrangement,'whaV ever it may be, by force,we shall have to prolong that control. This necessity is inherent with the situation. However, if the Lama persists in refusal to treat, the British Mission will have t°l make so protracted a stay that a Protectorate wil become virtual if not official. These are consider- tions of which Mr. BA.LYOUR would like to sal nothing whatever, and when they are discussed ill the House he will marvel still more at the Per' versity of the politician who will keep on saYing things so inconvenient to the Government, and e° distracting to the course of public business.

"Warrington is a smelly place" we heard some- (3o.e.say disgustedly the other day, and though the criticism touched our town-pride it was beyond refutation. With canals all round, a river through the centre, and Sankey Brook mendering many- Coloured along the western boundary, the ancient borough is certainly " high "at this time of the Year. This much of a nuisance is unavoidable so far as the town authorities are concerned, but there are parts of the town which might be made eeter. On Thursday for instance, the market 81o2ply reeked, though it was practically empty, and considerably more than "thirteen well-defined a.nd different stinks " assailed the nostrils. A little circular tour from the bottom of the "Barley "entry to Corporation-street and back down the other side of the Fish Market provided a complete olfactory education. Presumably, Wed- 13sclay's, tenants had "left a lot o little things behind 'em" which were getting the worse for weal% At any rate every 'ew yards introduced the pedestrian to a new aroma which, even as a novelty, could not be en- dured for more than a moment. Lime-street corner brought salvation. Here an energetic workman Was prettily painting a "pub,' and the homely whiff of" turps" seemed like eau-de-Cologne. We don't want to be rude, but we should likA to suggest that the Markets Committee insists upon a more thorough scavenging and flushing of its Premises during the hot weather.

Constant dropping wears away the stone, but constant complaining has not yet stirred .the re- sponsible public bodies to action respecting the condition of Sankey Brook. During the long spell of hot weather, the foul-smelling stream has been at its worst, and it seems out of all reason that the inhabitants of the neighbourhood should have to tolerate such a nuisance. The Sankey Parish Councillors are now making an effort to bring about an improvement, but they have a big task before them. Chle of the members in particular is convinced that the Warrington Corporation are the worst °fienders, but in order to ascertain something definite, a deputation is to make investigations. Afterwards the District Council, as having control ,0f a large area through which the brook runs, will " pproached. In the past the policy 'las been to shift the burden of responsibility from one authority to another. It is high time that some real attempt was made to deal with the nuisance, and we hope the course which the Sankey repre• aßentatives are pursuing will result in relief to the 401)g-suffering residents. Unfortunately, we can- not help having our doubts.

The British lion has been emitting sundry BaVage growls during the week over what has come to be termed "the Malacca incident," and with very little more provocation he would have linsheathed his claws, It is astonishing how truculent a nation we are. No sooner does some- -000 tread on our mythical but revered lion's tail than we are up in arms. Judging by the tone Of prevalent opinion during the week the people of this country are" jest spiliu' for a fite," to use a Yankeeism. The fact that only two years ago we e.cluandered 20,000 lives and £250,000,000 of money le Subjugating the Boers and introducing Chinese „4abour into their country seems to have "'eau overlooked in the general resentment tgainst the high-banded Russian seizure of the 4-• and 0. steamship Malacca. The national blo taa up in a minute, and we were talking of the 41e.diterranean fleet going here and there and .teing this and that before Russia could say the Lull' equivalent for "Jack Robinson." Fortun- tslY the tension has been relieved. According to 41,euter " The Russian reply to the British pro- ,test agrees that the Malacca shall not be brought ?ef9re a prize court,and undertakes that no similar incident shall occur in future.' So our amour -roPre ih saved. But is it not remarkable how 'eat?, as a nation,we are to put up our fists?

The break-up of the Ministry on the tariff rieetion is unmistakably proceeding at an expe- Rt,9d rate. The emphatic declaration of Lord 'allsbury against any proposal to impose a tax on 4e.°Lria means, of course, that he is totally opposedito rllll6 main plank of the Chamberlain platform. Oa eother hand,botk the Marquis of Lansdowne and 'he Earl of Selborne have formally made corn,non taut% with Mr. Chamberlain by joining the ecutive !Committee of the newly constituted 44:beral-Unionist Council, which exists now for the 13111Vose of prom )ting the policy which, according ° Mr. Chamberlain himself, cannot be carried °ll.t without a tax on corn. As the " Standard toints out, the action taken by the Foreign Secr.o- -q and the find Lord of the Admiralty is in mlrect opposition to the policy of the Government .declared by Mr. Balfour. A situation so corn- Pheated cannot endure much longer.

LOCAL NEWS.

Coming Events. To-day the meetings of S )uth West Lancashire Spiritualist Union will be held at the Warrington Spiritualist Hall. There will be a tea and public meeting in the evening. Addresses and clairvoyance will be given by Mrs. Batie and Mr. Wilkinson, of. Halifax, and Mr. Parr, president of the National Union Services will be held to-morrow and on Monday evening. At Wycliffe Church to-morrow (Sunday, the preachers will be the Rev. J. Yonge in the morning, and the Rev. W. Young, WA, district secretary of the Religious Tract Society, in the evening. The Rev. H. Dunn= will occupy his pulpit to-morrow for the last time until the latter part of September. , An open meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society will be held to-morrow (Sunday) afternoon. Mrs. Yonge will preside and Mrs. Moores will be the speaker. There will be instrumental selections by Master T. Barton and Master Pearce.

„ Warrington Secondary Day School Cycle Excursion.

The above school signalised "Breaking-up Day ." July 15th, by indulging in another outing on wheels. A party of 25 cyclists left the Technical Institute about 1.30 p.m. en route for Knutsford, via Latch- ford. Several of the staff accompanied the scholars. After resting at Knutsford, the journey was con- tinued to Arley, where tea had been arranged for. A couple of hours was profitably spent in the surround- ing country, and the return journey being commenced about 7.30, the school was again reached,shortly be- fore nine, thus terminating a must sucuessful and enjoyable excursion.

• The Howley Ward Elections. On Monday next palling will take place to fill the vacancy in Howley Ward occasioned by the elevation of.Councillor John Evans to an aldermanic seat. The candidates nominated are Messrs. Jacob Sankey (C) dairyman, 21, Church-street, and Thomas Chantler (Independent Non-Trader), beerhouse keeper, 92 Mersey-street. Several additional nomination papers have been handed in since Friday, the proposers and seconders being :—.On behalf of Mr. Ch.antler, Mr. Thomas Walsh and Mr. Thomas Pemberton ; Mr. William A. Leslie and Councillor Harroeks ; Mr. Charles Roddy and Mr. Patrick William Roddy ; Mr. William Malone and Mr. Arthur Owen. On behalf of Mr. Sankey, Councillor D wie3 and Mr. William Roberts.

Poor Children's Summer Camp. Looking bronzed and healthy. a detachment of 85 Warrington boys returned to town yesterday morning after a week of unalloyed pleasure at the little Welsh resort of Prestatyn. They owed their holiday to the thoughtfulness and generosity of many towns- people, who are doing their best to make the poor children's summer camp an annual red- letter week for the boys and girls who most need the change. From all accounts, the sojourn under canvas has been most agreeable. Each day some fresh form of recreation has been indulged in, and cricket and even football have been in geat favour. Then the lads have had the advantage of a daily dip in the sea, and on several occasions profit- able rambles have been organized. Prestatyn Moun- tain was climbed on Monday, and on Wednesday Dyseth was visited, the boys being delighted with the beautiful country. A kind-hearted landlady upon whom they called for a drink of water was generous enough to give each of them a bottle of lemonade. It is interesting to note the daily routine : —rise at 7 ,• breakfast, 8,30 ; dinner, 12; tea, 5; supper, 8.15 ; and. bed, 8.45. On Sunday the boys attended their respec- tive places of worship. Yesterday afternoon another contingent of 85 boys departed for Prestatyn in high spirits. Eighty five more will take their places when they return next Friday, and the week following the girls will have a turn. The officers at present in charge of the camp are: Officers-in-charge, Mr. Fred J. Emson ; assistant officers, Messrs. F. H. Aukland, A. Malcolm, G. Dawson, Alec. Chappell and Inspector Hackett. The cook is Mr. McFarlane, of Liverpool, who has had much experience in camp life. Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins and Mr. A. H. Lee have rendered invalnable services as secretary and treasurer respectively.

The Examiner, Saturday, July 23, 1904

Latchford Baptists at Frodsham. On Tuesday, the L itehford Baptist Sunday School had its annual trip to Frodsham, leaving Latchford Station at 9.30 a.m. and returning at 8.20 pan. About 250 scholars and friends made up the party. The weather was superb and a very enjoyable day was spent.

Local Band Success. Another success was achieved by the League of the Cross Band on Saturday. Competing at Bromboro' Pool, near Birkenhead, they carried off the first prize in the open competition against nine other bands. Mr. T. Hynes was the conductor, and the test piece " Recollections of Flotow."

July Fairs. The July cattle fair was held on. Monday on. the vacant land behind the Blue Coat School. Only 52 animals were on sale, and average prices were realized At the horse fair in the same place, on Tuesday, 101 animals were on the ground. The quality was rather better than in previous years. Business, however, was very slow,and only poor prices were forthcoming. The Manchester Ship Canal Bill. _ _ _

The litnchester Ship Canal Bill was considered by a committee ofthe House of Co,n.nans yesterday. The Mayor of the Borough (Alderman Bolton), the Town Clerk and the Borough Surveyor were in attendance on behalf of the Corporation. There was no opposi- tion to the Bill.

Hynes, to whin great credit is due. BaCE BOOTS FOR ALL.—UNDER THE CLOCK.—Advt Cricket League for Wipt Lancashire. A representative meeting of the various cricket clubs in West Lancashire was held at the Patten Arms Hotel on Tuesday evening, to consider the advisability of forming a league for next season. Mr. Emmett (Earlestown) was elected chairman of the meeting. Representatives of the following clubs attended : Warrington, Wigan, Earlestown, Newton-le-Willows, Prescot, St. Helens, St. Helens Recreation, Widnes and Leigh. All of the clubs with the exception of Wigan signified their intention of joining the league, and it is very probable that Wigan will also join. There was a general feeling that the formation of the league would tend to increase interest in the game. After discussion it was resolved to form a league under the title of the West Lancashire League. It was further resolved that the league should be limited to ten clubs in the first league and ten in the second league. A further meeting will be held on the 26th inst. Music in the Park.
