Bible -Class at Budworth. On Sunday a party of about 40 men, chiefly com- posed of members of the senior class of the Bank Quay Workingmen's Mission, with a few friends, visited Great Budworth to spend a pleasant Sunday evening in response to an invitation from Captain G. Th. Crosfield. The party left Warrington at 3.20, arriving at Great Budworth at 5 o'clock an excellent tea being provided at Mrs. Jackson's Temperance Hotel after which, sacred music was rendered by several of the party. After this they adjourned to the side of the lake where a profitable meeting was held. Captain Crosfield read "Ivan Ivanovitch " from Browning and after pointing out the valuable lessons to be learnt from the poem a very interesting conver- sation on the subject by the members brought the meeting to a close. The party left Great B adworth about 8,30 arriving back shortly after 9.30. The general opinion of those present was that it would be a great blessing to the thousands in our towns if it was arranged so that more services took place out in the open fields.

FIII3LIC BILNEFIT, UNDER the CLOCK for BEST BocTßin LANCASHIRE.—Advt. A Crimean Hero. The King has conferred the medal for meritorious service upon Sergeant-Major J. Nunnerley, late of the D Troop, Lancashire Hussars, and formerly of the 17th Lancers. The recipient of the decoration is a Warrington man, having been born at Wilderspool, and was the seventh son of Mr. Richard Nunnerley, well known in Warrington who was a native of Shropshire, and served in the Yeomanry Cavalry of that county. Of his seven brothers, two joined the Army, a third entered the Navy and some of the others joined the Volunteers. Sergeant- Major Nunnerley served in the Crimean campaign and was one of the gallant'. Six Hundred" who took part in the Balaclaya Charge. On leaving the Army he obtained a situation as stationmaster at Disley, near Buxton, but shortly afterwards, on the imitation of Lord Skelmersdale, becamedrill instructor to the D Troop of the Lancashire HusSars, and took up his residence at Ormskirk, where he now resides. In addition to the medal he has just received, he has been decorated with the Crimean medal with four clasps,the Turkish medal, and the F rench medal and diploma. Concert. . A. meeting of the Workingmen's Committee which is assisting to raise funds for the local war memorial was held in the Town Hall on Thursday evening. In the unavoidable absence of Mr. D. PRIMROSE Pregic.—On Saturday afternoon some of the wardens and sub-wardens and their wives and friends had a successful picnic to Oulton Park, the seat of Sir Philip de Grey Egerton. Mr. Dallow had made admir- able arrangements. THE OPEN AIR SERVICES.—The weather having been favourable several of these services have been held on Sunday evenings and have been, on the whole, well attended. They are held under the Big Tree in Red- lane, and at the bottom of the chapel steps on the broad open space. The Rev. J. S. Hughes conducted the service on Sunday evening last, and preached on "How to live," taking as his teat Titus h. 12. Special hymn papers were provided and the hymns were heartily sung. The service lasted rather less than an hour and was evidently enjoyed. WALKING DAY.—Yesterday witnessed the annual children's festival in the village known as Walking Day. The village was gay with flags/and bunting and the beautiful fine weather contributed to the success of the festival. In the early afternoon, the children attended service in St. Thomas's Church, when the Vicar (Rev. H. V. Pigot) officia'ed. Immediately after the service, the children formed in processional order, and formed a charming spectacle as they made a circui- tous tour through the village. The Blue Coat Band headed the procession and the clergy in attendance were the Rev. H. V. Pigot and the Rev. C. A. M. Evans (curate). The infants afterwards adjourned to the Church InstitUte for tea and the older scholars were entertained at the schools. Altogether about 1,000 children were catered for. After tea, an adjournment was made to the Brewery Park where the scholars dis- ported themselves and competed for prizes in athletic contests.

DEATH AND FUNERAL OF MRS. BIRTLES.

We announce with regret, which will be shared by very Many of our readers, the death of Mrs. Birtles, wife of Mr. Thomas Birtles, photographer, of Legh House Studio, who passed away at her residence, Brant- wood, Fearnhead, on Monday. The deceased lady had had a very brief illness,for it was only on the previous Tuesday that Dr. Joseph was called in. Her condition became so critical that on Wednesday the whole of the family were summoned. There was a slight rally on Sunday, when prayers for her recovery were offered at Christ Church, Padgate, the Parish Church, and Wycliffe Congregational Church. The deceased lady had attended the last-named place of worship. On Monday Mrs. Birtles had a relapse and passed away at 12.30 in the afternoon. For her pleasant and sympathetic disposition, and many good qualities, the deceased lady was held in esteem by a large circle of friends who received the news of her death with profound • regret. She was 68 years of age.—The funeral took place at Hill Cliffe graveyard on Thursday afternoon and was attended by the relatives and a few old friends. A short service was conducted at the house by the Rev.'John Yonge, and the corbSge left for the graveyard at 11.30. The last rites were per- formed by the Rev. John Yonge who was accompanied by the Rev. W. Pollock Hill. vicar of Padgate. On the coffin was placed a beautiful wreath from the members of the family. It consisted of crimson ramblers and roses from the deceased lady's garden. Other wreaths were sent by :—Mr. and Mrs. Arthur Browne, Dorothy and Norman, Mr. and Mrs. L. Birtles, Mr. and Mrs. James C. Bate, Roger and Basil, Mr. and Mrs. J. Foster Jackson. Mr. W. H. Grimsdale (Twickenham), Edith and Mabel Grimsdale, Mildred Jackson, Mr. and Mrs. T. lace, Mrs. Longshavv and family, Rev. J. and Mrs. Yonge, Rev. W. and Mrs. Pollock-Hill, Mr. and Mrs. Roger Bate, Mr. and Mrs. Warwick Brookes (Manchester), Mrs. Peter Leigh and family, Mr. and Mrs. W. S. Howard, Mr. and Mrs. Main- waring and family, Mr. and Mrs. William Gil i and family, Mr. and Mrs. William Richardson, Mr. and Mrs. .Tames Richardson, Mr. and Mrs. John E. Richardson, Mr. and Mrs. John Bennett and family, Mrs. T. Tunstall and family, Mrs. and Miss Shaw Green, Mr. and Mrs. Henry Roberts, Mr. and Mrs. R. Silcock and family: Mr. and Mrs. A. Silcock. SOCIAL SERMONETTES FOR WORKING MEN.

BY JOHN TAYLOR. Author of Search Lights on Land, Labour and Social Problems."

===== 0002601_19040723_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
LEGISLATION WITHOUT MANDATE.

"If you proceed to change the spirit and the method in which the laws are made, depend upon it, you will find a change in the spirit in which the people of the country obey the laws and reverse the Parliament that makes them. If you persist in mak- ing laws without the full and free discussion, which has given the people of this country confidence in the laws,and respect for those who administer them, you will lose that element in our national life which has made us the most law-abiding of people, and has given our institutions a stability possessed by no other."— JOHN MORLEY.

Big majorities, and long terms of office, and aristo- cratic rules, are not the best things that can come to us. We have had nearly nine years of Tory rule, with earls, and dukes, and nobles, holding the highest offices in the State, and what is our condition to-day ? The Government has quarrelled with some of the wisestand best of its supporters, and it has kicked out of office those who had brains and conscience enough to warn it of the errors of its ways. It is with the ut- most difficulty that, the Tory members can be induced to respond to the crack of the party whip, and the Prime Minister has been howled down, and refused a hearing, while the" Mother of Parliaments "has been turned Into a bear garden. And what does the country think of its legislators? Every bye-election pronounces against them, and either elects an opponent, or sends a supporter with a greatly reduced majority. Thousands of our best and most loyal citizens are in open mutiny against some of the laws they have made. The Passive Resisters, not merely refuse to pay their rates, but they "cheerfully submit to the spoiling of their goods" and gladly go to gaol for their conscience sake.

Do you ask me why the Government is so cordially hated, and detested by so many of its best citizens, and why men openly defy the laws that it has passed. It is because they have lost the confidence of the country, and,they are legislating now without any mandate from the electors,and in defiance of the wishes of the people. Every bye-election is a protest against their despotic and tyrannical legislatin, I.—THE TORIES CAME INTO OFFICE BY FALSE

===== 0002601_19040723_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
PRETENCES.

Nine years ago they promised us an era of peace and of do mestic reforms if we wluld vote for them. They said they could form a strong and stable Government, that would command the respect of all other nations, then "wars would cease to the ends of the earth." They told us that the money saved by their peaceful policy would give us old age pensions and find plenty of work for our labourers, and give us decent homes for working mai. How have they kept their promises? Did jihey give us an era of peace? No, they plunged us into thirteen or fourteen wars in nine years, and every one of these wars might have been avoided. Did they give us old age pensions? Well they gave a big pension to the late Duke of Cambridge to bribe him out of the War Office. They gave an enormous grant to Lord Roberts, for soothing our nerves by telling us that the war in South Africa was over, and they gave another big grant to Lord Kitchener for finishing the work that " Bobs " had left undone. They squandered millions of money on the land-owners and the parsons, but they had not a copper to spare for the working men, who had given them their votes because they believed their lying promises. In 1900 we had a khaki election, and the Tories were returned to finish the war, and restore order in South Africa. They told us it was a miners' war, and there would be plenty of employment at high wages for white men in the gold and diamond mines when the country was settled. They promised equal rights for all the people and self-government for every citizen. How have they kept their promises? They handed over the conquered territory to the tender mercies of the Colonial Office, and they sanctioned the importation of thou- sands of Chinese slaves, who earn thirty shillings a month. So the British working man who shed his blood and squandered his money in conquering the territory, is now elbowed out of it, to enable the mine-owners to make enormous dividends out of slave labour.

So you see on two separate occasions the Tories have won elections by lying and false pretences, and they have made a good thing out of their dishonesty. If a poor starving wretch tells you a lie, and gets sixpence or a shilling out of you, because you believed his false- hoods, you can run him in and imprison him for obtain- ing money by false pretences. But we have one law for the big sinners and another for the little sinners. A political trickster who cannot afford to keep a conscience, and who boasts, that he has an open mind, may ' beguile you with pro- mises that he never intends to fulfil. He may lie for all that he is worth and you will make him a Cabinet Minister at a salary of one hundred pounds a week. And when you have put him into office by your votes,he will turn and rend you and defy you to put him out again.

11.-THE PRIME MINISTER HAS BECOME OUR MASTER INSTEAD OF' OUR SERVANT.

The electors in 1900 gave lxim a big majority "to put an end to the war, and to restore order in South Africa," and he is using the majority he got for that task for very different purposes. If he had told us four years ago that he intended to destroy our Schoolßoards and water down our education system, and fling our fiscal system into the melting pot, and increase our taxation, and introduce Chinese slaves into South Africa, and endow the rich brewers, who own tied houses, and gag and muzzle the House of Commons, the country would have voted very differently. All these subjects are burning questions of a highly controversial character, and he ought to have consulted the country about them before he had rushed 'them through his khaki House of Commons. Gladstone and Disraeli never legislated without mandate, and in defi- ance of the wishes of the electors, and they never tampered with the liberties of the House of Commons as Mr. Balfour has done. If he had carried on these games two or three centuries ago, the next House of Commons would have impeached him, and tried him for his life on a charge of treason against the liberties of the people of England. We have had high-handed tyraats of Mr. Balfonr's type before, but our fore- fathers would not stand any of their tyranny. The Duke of Wellington was probably one of the worst Prime Ministers this country ever had. He knew what the people of England did not want so he tried to cram the thing they hated down their throats. They knew what they did want, and he did everything in his power to prevent them from getting it. He so enraged and ex- asperated the people that they mobbed him and spat on him when he went into the streets, and he had to be protected by .a bodyguard of troops. He wanted to plant cannon m Whitehall to overawe the people and shoot them down if they came too near his sacred per- son. But when the next election came and the people had a chance of voting,they made short work of his pre- tensions and banished him from office for ever.

In the days of Charles 1., Lord Strafford and Archbishop Laud defied the wishes of the people till they were arrested and impeached and tried on a charge of "treason against the liberties of the people in those stormy times." Men were hanged and beheaded in those days for far less crimes than he has committed, and if he, had the same liberty-loving people to deal with now, they would impeach him and put an end to his mischievous and aanprous antics. Our Parliamentary rules were originally framed on the supposition that the Prime Ministers would always be the servants of the State and that they would never try to be its masters. It was taken for granted that they would always be honourable men, and that they would resign as soon as they lost the confidence of the nation. It was also expected that they would be men who loved righteousness and hated oppression. So the law declares that any Parliament may be elected for seven years, and at the end of that period it must be dissolved whether it is defeated or not. Bat there is an understanding among all parties in the State that if the Ministry is out-voted and defeated on any Government question it shall resign, and appeal to the people, and if any great measure of reform is proposed., the matter shall first of all be fought out in the con- stituencies.

I Mr. Balfour has not been loyal and true to the tradi- tions of the House of Commons. He has introduced I revolutionary measures like the Education Bill, and his Licensing Bill, and his Chinese Labour ordinance, not only without the knowledge and consent of his con- stituents, but in opposition to the wishes of the majority 'of the people. He has not allowed ample time for the , full discussion of these highly controversial subjects, but he has forced them through the Ilc;use of Commons by the gag, and closure, and coercion. If he thinks he is going to use his khaki •majority to pass unpopular measures like his Licensing and Educa- • tion Bills,and to place them permanently on the Statute Book, he is grievously mistaken. The nation is getting tired of seeing him plundering the taxpayers of this country of millions of pounds every year, and dividing the plunder among his Tory friends and supporters. Sir Henry Campbell Bannerman has already warned him ' that he must not expect the next Liberal Government ,to accept his gagged and closured Acts of Parliament I as Mud settlements of these great questions. Mr. lalfour sticks to office like grim death to a dead nigger. suppose he is waiting to be kicked out. He does not like the thoughts of parting with his hundred pounds a week job,and he does not care to lose his posi- tion and power in the State. And he probably thinks that if he sticks to office a few years longer the nation will forget his dealings with public- ans, and parsons, and pigtails, and lose sight of his high crimes and misdemeanours before the reckoning day. Let him harbour no such delu- sions Some of us have long memories. t.

===== 0002601_19040723_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

Local enthusiasts will hail with satisfaction the decision to form a West Lancashire League. It has long been felt that such an organization was needed to arouse a general interest in the sport in Warrington, and there should be some large gates at Arpley next season. Theaverage spectator likes to know that the opposing teams are fighting for positions in a league. The fact in itself adds infinitely more zest to the games. The West Lancashire League will include many well known elevens in the neighbour- hood, and keen struggles for honours are anticipated. Should the league be successful,—and there is every in- dieation that it will be—Warrington will be able to congratulate themselves upon having played a pro- minent part in its formation. •

Warrington's victorious career received an unpleasant check on Saturday, To Sefton belongs the distinction of having inflicted the first defeat of the season, and, surprising as it may seem, the reverse lacked nothing in completeness. The Warringtonians appeared to be quite off colour, and allowing their opponents to compile 204, could only make 59 in re- sponse. Of course, even the best elevens cannot expect to be always victorious, but ;Warrington have done so surprisingly well that it was disappointing to have to bend the knee to Sefton. The result was all the more acceptable to the Liverpool contingent as they were beaten by Warrington in the opening match of the season. I hope our wielders of the willow will be in better form to-day, and that they will emerge trium: phant from their encounter with Earlestown away.

The Sefton batsmen were in a merry mood, and there was some tall scoring. A. H. Boswell gave a brilliant display and knocked up 56, whilst H. Blease was nearly as good with 51. H. Tongue was responsible for 47. The bowling of Lynes was the most effactive,his capture being 8 wickets for 86 runs.

It was most unusual to see the Warrington side cut up so poorly. In a little over an hour they were all dismissed, Kitchener and Boswell bowling splendidly. Kitchener took 6 wickets for 29 runs, and performed the "h at trick," whilst Boswell eaptured 4 wickets at a cost of 29. ,

The second eleven drew with Sefton at Arpley. Batting first, the visitors compiled 167, the feature of the innings being the vigorous hitting of J. Harrison whose efforts produced 73. Thewlis and J. Thompson did good work with the ball, and took five wickets for 44 runs, and three for 19 respectively. At the call of time, Warrington had lost 8 wickets for 131, L. R. Connor having amassed 54. His hits included nine boundary strokes.

St. Paul's made a successful visit to Helsby, defeat- ing the villagers by 17. J. I,Varl was their top scorer his innings realizing 29. A. Cooke knocked up 16 for Helsby.

Latchford Baptists, thanks in a great measure to the assistance of Lester and Hunt, accounted for Pemberton St. Cutherts. The home team had first turn at the wicket and compiled 65, the visitors being all out for 42

Cheshire Lines suffered a heavy reverse at the hands of Orford Barracks. The military men had an easy task for they got all the railway men out for 52, and then scored 203 for the loss •f only 5 wickets. Their top scorer was Corporal Meade (43 not out), J. Duckworth (21) making the best display for thp losers.

Bewsey-road Wesleyans were in poor form against the " Guardian " printers who achieved the victory by a 46 margin. Geldered(2o) was the most successful bats- man.

===== 0002601_19040723_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
Bowling.

44Congratulations to"P. and K." No. 1! They have played splendidly throughout the season, and richly deserve to win the Finlayson Cup. The final on Saturday was a rare struggle, but Cheshire Lines had to admit defeat. It was with genuine pride that George Ball carried off the trophy on behalf of his excellent team, and he generously expressed the hope that next year the Rail- way men would be triumphant. I can only re-echo the sentiment, for the team made a brave bid for the cup on Saturday. The officials and com- mittee are deserving of a meed of praise for the admir- able arrangements which resulted in the contest passing off without the slightest hitch. A full deqcripticn of the games appears in another column.

There was a jovial gathering at the King and Queen on Thursday evening, when Mr. Fred Clare, the popular landlord, entertained the teams and officials at tea. Afterwards an enjoyable ' smoker" was held, the event of the evening being the presentation of a pipe to George Ball, the captain of the winning twelve. The gift had been subscribed for by the members of the team, and was handed to Mr. Ball by Mr. G. L. Brime- low. The recipient was quite taken by surprise, and modestly assured the company that he had done no more than any other player to bring about the success of "P. and K." Songs were rendered by Messrs. W. Skelhorne, G. Higham, F. Colburn, J. Horsfteld and F. W. Scarisbrick, and the gramaphone selections by Mr. Smallman were much appreciated. A cordial vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. Clare for his hospitality. Mr. Samuel Taylor made an admirable chairman.

To-day the match "Winners v. Rest " will be decided and the proceeds are to be given to the Infirmary and Nursing Association. The following team has been selected to represent" the rest" :—George Ball (cap- tain), Thewlis's ; George Holbrook, Rylands's ; Hermon Williams, Firth Company; Arthur Gleave, Whitecross ; James Holland, London and North Western Railway; William Hill, Houghton's; Arthur Shaw, Rylands's; Thomas Ellison, Whitecross ; John Monks, Cheshire Lines ;William Andrews, Orford Tannery; H. Daintith, Smith's Wire Works ; H. Fogg, Stubbs's File works. This is a formidable combination, and George Ball, junior, and his merry men will have tireir work cut out to win.

At a meeting of the Warrington and District League held at the Brooklands on Thursday evening, under the presidency of Mr. G. F. Freeman, the team to repre- sent the league in the return match with the Widnes and Runcorn League was selected exactly the same as in the first engagement. The match will take place at Halton on Thursday next at four o'clock.

-- Two league matches were played on Saturday. Horse and Jockey performed a smart feat in beating Cemetery by 18, and as may be concluded there was great excitement. Some of the games were close, and it is worthy of note that J. Berry, who had accomplished the best individual performance up to Saturday, was beaten by T. Wells by 15-8. The Liberal Club sustained one more defeat, the victprs being Sankey Chapel House. A particularly close contest was that in which E. J. Mitchell and J. Barton figured, Mitchell triumphing by the odd point.

Penketh Park trounced their friends from Sankey on Thursday, winning ten qf the twelve games, and com- piling a majority of 69. ' As the result indicates, there were very few close contests.

The following is the league table up to date : P. W. L. For Agst. Pts. Cemetery Horse and Jockey lO ... 6 ... 4 ...1542 ...1282 ... 12 Bull's Head Chapel House Fearnhead Brooklands Penketh Liberal Club

To-day's league matches are : Bull's Head v. Horse and Jockey. Chapel House V. Cemetery. Liberal Club v. Brooklands. Brooklands v. Penketh Park.

The Liberal Club main for the prizes generously pre- sented by Mr. A. H. Crosfleld will take place on August 6th. The prizes will be distributed at a smoker. to be held at the close of the season, and the donor will be asked to preside.

On the same date the second bowling handicap in connection with the Prince of Wales Lodge of Odd. fellows will take place on the Brooklands green. There is every prospect of a big entry and some capital bowling for the cup and medal presented by Mr. A. H. Crosfleld.

Cycling.

The Warrington Club's run on Saturday was to Hunt's Cross. The weather was glorious;but as many members were away for holidays only fourteen turned out. On arrival tea was served at the Hunt's Cross Hotel, and afterwards the bowling green was placed at the disposal of the party. Several pleasurable games were witnessed. After a pleasant ride,Warrington was reached in good time, and the outing may be classed as one of the most enjoyable of the season.

Nearly a score of members made the journey to. Warburton on Tuesday evening although the weather at the outset was unpromising. The pretty village was reached via Lymru, and bowling was indulged in at the Saracen's Head. The return run was made via Warbur- tonßridge and Manchester Road, the outing having proved most successful.

--- To-day's run is to Davenham,and on Tuesday the des- tination will be Hoo Green.

Prosecutions for "scorching" are Seldom heard at, local courts, but at the County Petty Sessions on Wed- nesday several riders were dealt with for the offence. They were seen going at a furious and reckless rate in Lumber-lane, Collin's Green, and in turning a corner ran on to the footpath. The defendants, whose names were

Walter Moore, John Ball, David Baines and James Smith, were fined ss. and costs.

Water Polo.

There were two exciting matches at the Corporation baths last evening. Warrington second met Everton third,and the contest resulted in a draw of one goal each. The first team tried conclusions with Everton second, and at half time were leading by two goals to one. The final result was a victory for Warrington by three goals to two. W. Ashton (2) and A. Ashton were the scorers for Warrington.

The secend round of the works squadron competition was decided. Pearson and Knowles' defeated Fletcher Russell's, Crosfield's accounted for the Co-operative Stores, and Rylands's were victorious over Richmond's.

THE EDUCATION ACT. The Stockton Heath Passive Resisters.

INTERESTING PROCEEDINGS.

The passive resisters question at Stockton Heath has reached an interesting stage. It will be remem- bered as already reported in our columns that the Revs,H. Dunnico, J. S. Hughes aLd Mr. T. Law were summoned before the magistrates atDaresbury to shew cause &c., why they did ir)t pay their poor rate. As thelpoor] rate included the so-called education rate they stated they had already paid what they ,consid- ered was due from them as citizens for poor rate but declined on conscientious groilnds to pay the propor- tion which they considered was being used under the Education Act, for sectarian religious education. However the magistrates declined to recognise con- scientious objection as a valid objection and made the usual order for payment. That was Act No. 1. The next stage of the proceedings or rather to be correct the next expected proceeding was the seizure of the goods of the passive resisters—as the latter declined to obey the order for payment. But although the mighty law is behind-them, so far, no one can be found to seize the goods. A deadlock has therefore ensued. This is Act No. 2.

Certain of the local authorities say it is not, their province to make the distraint. The police have been asked, but they state it is out of their jurisdiction at present. Various rumours of the reason of the appar- rent deadlock are in circulation in Stockton Heath. One is that no one likes to put an unpleasant proceed- ing in force against such highly respectable and use- ful men as the Revs. H. Dunnico, J. S. Hughes, and Mr. T. Law. Another is, that although these gentle- men are passive resisters on this matter, yet they will be bold enough to express their opinions probably after the sale takes place, and throw some light on the Education Act, and it is stated there are people in Stockton Heath who prefer at present to keep educa- tional matters quiet, as the parishioners may have their eyes opened. In any event arrangements have been made to hold a public meeting in Victoria-square immediately after the sale. But up to the time of writing no one as yet,has made a visit to the residences of the passive resisters. That will be Act No. 3. And rumour further states that when the officials of the law do make a visit, that in one case at least, there will be no goods to seize, as occurred in the Rev. J. F. Matthews' case at Latchford.

WARRINGTON MILK PROSECUTION.

Fails on a • Technical Point.

George Robinson. farmer, Hatton, was summoned at the Warrington Police Court yesterday for having on June 29th sold milk from which 10 per cent. of cream had been abstracted.—Mr. Stanley Wilson deputy town clerk, prosecuted for the Health Committee, and Mr. Brown p defended.

Mr. Wilson said Inspector Flood was on duty in John- street at 7.30 a.m. on the date in question, and saw a boy named Frank Mines, in the employ of the defend- ant, delivering a tankard of milk to a dairy. The inspector purchased a quart of new milk from the lad, and a sample was submitted to the public analyst (Mr. F. G. Ruddock) who certified that 10 per cent of cream had been abstracted.

--Inspector Flood produced the analyst's certificate. Mr. Ruddock stated that he based his opinion that 10 per cent. of cream had been abstracted on the fact that the sample contained only 2.69 per cent, of fat, whereas normal milk contained at least 3 per cent. Witness was cross-examined at length as to why he did not visit the defendant's farm and take further samples in accordance with the suggestion of the Bench in a previous case. He said he did not consider it necessary to visit the farm when the sample he had taken came direct from Mr. Robinson. Mr. Browne then pointed out that the Act said dis- tinctly that the certificate of analysis must be in the form provied in schedule of the statute. According to that schedule the analyst had to insert in the certificate the name of the person submitting the sample. The present certifi- cate was headed "to the Health Committee of the Corporation of Warrington," whereas the sample was submitted by inspector •Flood. Mr. Browne produced certificates given by Dr. Gornall which were addressed to Inspector Flood. He quoted a case in which a conviction had been quashed because the certificate was informal. if the Bench ruled against him he would have no hesitation in advising Mr. Robin- son.to appeal.

The ni,igistrates held that the informality was not sufficient to make the objection fatal. They were will- ing to state a case.

The Deputy Clerk (Mr. E. A. White) intimated that the Bench would like to hear Mr. Wilson with regard to Clause 4of the Act under which proceedings were taken: "In the case of a certificate with regard to milk, butter or any article liable to decomposition, the analyst shall specially report whether any change had taken place in the constitution of the article that would inter- fere with the analysis." He did not see any such report on Mr. Ruddock's certificate.

Mr. Wilson said it had been held in a case where a summons was alleged to be defective in the particulars given, that it was for the justices to decide if the particulars were sufficient. He submitted that the Bench had a discretionary power to admit or reject the certificate. He would point out that the sample was delivered to the analyst on the same day that Inspector Flood took it from the cart, and the analyst's certificate was received the following day. He should think it was hardly necessary to have a certificate that the milk was not sour.

Mr. Browne contended that the point was abso- lutely fatal. The Bench could not override the Act of Parliament.

After the Bench had consulted in private, the Chair- Man said they were of opinion that the form in which the certificate was made out was not a sufficient com- pliance with the instructions, and was fatal tc) the prosecution. It appeared to the magistrates to be a matter of regret that an important case of that kind should have to be dismissed on a technical point. It would have been far better in everybody's interests that it should have been fought out on its merits. Mr. Browne applied for costs. The Chairman intimated that the Bench had already decided not to allow any. Mr. Browne said his client instructed him that he had never touched the milk. He had no separator or apparatus of that kind.

The Earl of Craven is about to stock the waters of his Berkshire estate, Hamstead Marshall, near Newbury, with trout. He is therefore having all the coarse fish removed, and some hundred weights have been taken from the waters, including a mammoth pike weighing about 301bs.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

Warrington Borough Police Court.

Minor Cases.

MONDAY.—Before the Mayor (in the chair), Alderman Smethurst, Councillors Thornton, Tinnion, and Wil- kinson, and Messrs. Birettall, Wright, William Roberts,

and Woods. , THE STRANGE CONDUCT OF A GREENGROCER.—DanieI Taylor,Paul-street,was charged with having been drunk whilst in charge of a horse and cart in Arpley-road on July 2nd, add also with a similar offence on June 26th in Paul-street. He was further charged on two infor- mations with having furiously driven 3 horse in Paul- street and Wilderspool-road. He pleaded guilty to all the charges.—The Chief Constable reminded the magis- trates that the defendant was before the Court a fort- night ago, and was then sent to the Union in order that his state of mind might be inquired into. He had had an interview with Dr. Braide, who said the man was suffering from delirium tremens. Therefore he felt constrained to go on with the charges that morning without unduly pressing them, and to leave the matter entirely in their Worships' hands. He might say that the defendant had had some domestic troubles before he began his drinking "bout."—The magistrates fined defendant 10s. and costs (355. in all), or in default of payment 14 days' imprisonment. MISCELLANEOUS.—George Tingle, forgeman, 21, War- burton-street, was fined 7s. for fighting in Kerfoot-street on Julylst.—Annie Pritchard, 7, Garnett's yard, was ordered to pay ss, for having been disorderly in Gar- nett's-yard.--Ellen Price, Dakin's-yard, Church-street, was similarly dealt with for having made use of abusive language on July 12th.—George Drew, 3, Lomax-yard, was summoned for having illtreated a horse by working it in an unfit state on July 11th; and Arthur Lunt, 85, Howley-lane, for causing it to be worked.—lnspector Gibson, R.S.P.C.A., proved the case.—lt was stated that the Animal would be destroyed, and the magistrates, thereupon, ordered Lunt to pay the costs.— Thomas Mitchell,ll, Litherland's-yard,was charged with having assaulted his wife, on June 25th.—The magis- trates thought there were faults on both sides, and ordered defendant to pay the costs (16s. 6d.)—Thomas Farrington, 11, Erwood-street, who made his 16th appearance. was sant to gaol for a month for vagrancy. —Patrick Collins, 13, Arpley-walk, was ordered to pay is. a week towards the cost of the maintenance of his two daughters in an industrial school.—James Fenn, 6, Platt-street, was fined in the costs (12s. 6d.) for having travelled on the London and North-Western Railway from Wigan to Warrington without having paid his fare —Defendant said he had not been to Wigan. and caused some amusement by stating that he did not remember having seen the platform. TUESDAY.—Before Alderman Hutchinson (in the chair), and Messrs. C.. 1. Holmes, C. S. Burgess, and W. J.

Richardson. THEFT.—Abraham Dykes, 5, Richardson's-row,Sankey Bridges, was charged with stealing from a cabin at Messrs. Monks, Hall, and Co's works, between July 16th and 18th, a kettle and a saucepan, of the value of 4d.,the property of the company.—Mr. F. W. Monks, who ap- peared for the company, said he did not wish to press the case.—The magistrates.fined prisonerss., or in de- fault seven days' imprisonment. 'WEDNESDAY.—Before Aldermen Fairclough (in the chair) and Hutchinson, Messrs. H. Roberts, Birchall, Reynolds. F. W. Monks, and Jolley. THREE MONTHS FOR THEFT.—Henry Caffrey, farm labourer. was charged with stealing from the pawnshop of Annie Houghton,Mersey-street, apair of men's boots value ss.lld.—P.C.Dyson met the prisoner carrying the bootsunder hiscoat, and questioned him. Prisoner said he had bought them, but it was discovered that they had been stolen from the shop—Prisoner was sentenced to three months' hard labour. THURSDAY.—Before Messrs. Charles White (in the chair), F. W. Monks, J. R. Young, and W. Roberts. FAMILY DESERTION.—CharIes Melton, last from Don- caster, was charged on a warrant with deserting his wife and two children, leaving them chargeable to the common funds of the Union.—Mr. B. Shearer stated that the wife and children were admitted to the Work- house a fortnight ago. Prisoner had sent no money towards their maintenance, although he was in a position to pay. He had now made an offer to contribute 10s. a week and the costs.—This course was agreed to, and the case was adjourned for a month. ARRESTED ON SUSPICION.—Henry Anderson, a tramp, was charged with being found in possession of a travelling rug and failing to give a satisfactory account of the same.—The Chief Constable asked for a remand until to-day. in order that the police could make inquiries. Prisoner was seen by P.C. Turner in Liver- pool-road, and when asked how he came into possession of the rug, he failed to give satisfactory replies.—The remand was granted.

SPEECHES AT THE DINNER. The Council dined together in the evening, after the conclusion of the competitions, in the Assembly Rooms at the Lion Hotel. The catering of Mr. Lee gave every satisfaction.

Considerable interest is already being taken in the "team " race which is being organised by the redoubt- able Motor Cycling Club. Although it is not intended that this shall be run Until sometime towards the end of August, applications are coming in for full particu- lars. The event should prove one of the most interest- ing of the season.

Major Davies who presided, asked the Council to drink to the health of Chief Officer Gallon, who, he said, had pone to a great deal of trouble to ensure the success of the day's proceedings. They sincerely thanked him for what he had done, and hoped he would long continue his services to the Union. (Applause). In reply Mr. Gallon said it was a pleasure to him to know that the visit to Warrington had been such a pronounced success. He must ad- mit that his "boys " had worked very hard, but the success which had been achieved was chiefly due to Mr. Shaw, the hon. secretary. (Applause). He had only been the instrument in that gentleman's hands. To him was due the praise. If the Council were satisfied, he was,and he thought the directors of Messrs. Crosfield were. He thanked them very sincerely for the kind manner in which his toast had been received. (Applause.) Mr. NEWTON (treasurer), in very complimentary terms, proposed The Visiting Friends," coupled with the name of Captain George R. Crosfield. Captain CROSFIELD, in reply, said his regret was that. Dr. Markel was unable to be present, because it was due to his initiation and that of their energetic captain (Mr. Gallon) that his firm had taken the keenest interest in the competitions. He could assure them that his firm were fully alive to the very great advantages of large firms having their own fire brigades because it was in the first few moments that brigades, could be saved. While he did not say it was not necessary to have Corporation brigades, if they had an organization on the spot it could enormously help the Corporation brigade. (Hear, hear, and applause.) That was pretty evident. They had had a capital display that afternoon and the audience was most appreciative.He could assure the members of the Council that his firm would continue to take the greatest interest in the Union, which they thought was a most excellent institution and was doing a great amount of good throughout the country. He wished it success and anything Messrs. Crosfield and Sons could do as the years went on would be gladly done. (Applause.) The health of the Chairman tvas drunk, and the pro- ceedings terminated.

THE VOLUNTEER ENCAMPMENT.

Departure To-Day.

SUNDAY'S SERVICE AT ST. PAUL'S CHURCH

This evening the local Volunteer Battalion will leave Warrington for Perham Downs, Salisbury Plain, for the annual encampment. The advance party and transport section, numbering about fifty, made the long journey at midnight on Tuesday, and will now have made all arrangements for the reception of the main body. The officers in charge of the detachment were :—Major B. Fairclough, Captain Avery,. Lieutenant Ridgway, and Captain and Quarter-Master A. F. G. Potter. At 10.15 this morning the Mounted Infantry Company cooks, cyclists and officers' grooms, to the number of 100, under the direction of Captain Haslam, will depart for camp. The remainder of the battalion will parade at 6.15 this evening, and will proceed by two trains from Central Station to Salisbury Plain. Fitst train, Lieut. Colonel F. A. Edelsten, Captain and Adjutant A. F. Bundock, Sergt.Major J. Rawlinson, Regimental Staff, Band, A. B. C. D. E. Companies. Second train, Major J. D. Fairclough, Sergt-Instructors Allardice and Cleaver, F. G. H. 1. Companies, and odd details. G. Company will meet the battalion at the Drill Hall at 6 p.m. Dress for all parties Will be :—to carry carbines, non-commissioned officers and men marching order, with valises and forage caps. Helmet will not be taken to camp.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

LOCAL MINING SUCCESSES.

In the recent Science and Art, examinations held by the Board of Education, we find than Mr. Percy Ormand of Torisholme, Earlestown. student of the Mining and Technical College, Wigan, has obtained a first class in the Honours Stage of Mining, and has been awarded a bronze medal.

===== 0002601_19040723_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
THEFT AT WARRINGTON.

At Warrington County Petty Sessions on Wednesday, Laurence Dunn was fined 20s. and costs or fourteen days for stealing a coat and two pairs of spectacles, the property of William Henry Wilkinson, licensee of the Queen Anne public-house, Golborne. SUDDEN DEATH OF DR. HANDYSIDE. EARLESTOWN.

_ On Monday about one o'clock Dr. Handyside of Earlestown died very suddenly at the age of 62 years. The deceased gentleman was for many years the London and North-Western Railway Company's local surgeon, and was also medical officer for several Friendly Societies. He was a prominent Conservative, and interested himself greatly in the lectures promoted by the Newton-in-Makerfteld Lectures Committee, and his sudden demise has caused a great shock in the district. The funeral took place on Thursday afternoon, the interment being at the cemetery. There was a very large attendance of friends and relatives, and several gentlemen in the district sent their carriages. Numerous wreaths were also sent, these being conveyed to the cemetery in a special carriage.

SURPRISING REPORTS

FROM DIFFERENT PARTS OF ENGLAND OF CURES BY DODD'S KIDNEY PILLS.

Surprising reports continue to come from various parts of England of cures of different diseases by Dodd's Kidney Pills. The similarity of symptons in these different ailments indicate that they are all the result of bad kidney action and consequently can be treated by a purely kidney remedy such as Dodd's Kidney Pills claim to be.

At any rate, the latest report comes from Yorks, and appears to be well authenticated. It is that of Miss Ruth Calvert of Church-road, Goathland, Yorks. She, itappears, suffered for three years from Dropsy, Heart Disease, and Lumbago. That she is now in greatly im- proved health is assumed, and she herself gives all toe credit to Dodd's Kidney Pills. The young lady is quoted as saying : " For over three years I suffered from Dropsy and Heart Disease with Lumbago in my back. I was atten- ded by doctors and went to the hospital, but they did me no good. I was in this condition when I heard of Dodd's Kidney Pills and resolved to give them a trial, and I must say the result was. surprising. They have done me a great deal of good, giving me great relief from the first. lam sure anyone who suffers as I did should not hesitate to take Dodd's Kidney Pills."

PASSIVE RESISTANCE.

Rev. H. Dunnico Explains His

Appearance Before the Magistrates. At Golborne-street Baptist Church on Sunday even- ing the Rev. H. Dunnico preached to a crowded con- gregation on "Why I appeared before the magistrates." Basing his remarks on Acts v. 29, "We ought to obey God rather than man," the preacher said that on the Tues- day previous he had appeared for the first time in his life before the magistrates. His appearance there had been severely criticized, and even some of his friends did not altogether agree with the policy of passive resist- ance. He was entirely agreed with them that to resist the law was a dangerous thing to do under normal circumstances, and the man who did resist it at any time ought to be able to give the highest and the holiest reason for so doing. It was because he had those reasons he ha resisted, and it was in order that those reasons might be more widely known that he had chosen that particular sub- ject. He had decided to speak upon that subject under a strong sense of public duty and conscientious com- pulsion, and in the second place he was simply express- ing his own personal opinion. The majority present he knew, would agree with him, but svme would not. With the latter he must agree to differ, and he would respect their convictions as he trusted they would res- pect his. Why had he appeared before the magistrates? -According to his summons because he had" refused to pay his poor rate or any portion thereof." That was distinctly untrue. He had paid his poor rate—every penny of it ; what he had refused to pay was a rate levied for the support of sectarian schools mder private management, schools which avowedly existed for the purpose of teaching dogmas and doctrines which in his opinion were pernicious and detrimental to the highest welfare not only of the children, but of the nation, doctrines against which their very existence as Nonconformists was a standing and an historic protest. He was opposed to this Education Act as an education - alist, as a citizen,and because he was bitterly opposed to such a violation of English constitutional law as this Act committed; but it was not on those grounds that he was a passive resister. If it were merely a bad piece of legislation, which it certainly was, then they would work and hope for the dawn of a day when it should be repealed, but it was because this Act invaded the realm of conscience that they adopted the policy of civil disobedience. There lay the cause of the whole question. The Nonconformist who did one single thing to assist the carrying out of this act was unworthy of his profession and signed the death warrant of religious liberty. The speaker gave a lengthy account of the history of elementary education in this country,and repudiated 'the claims put forth by the church to be the pioneers in the movement and said that the Church as a church had never seen known to lift hand or foot in the interest of popular education except for clerical ends ;in fact the clergy had been the consistent oppon- ents of all progress. The pioneer of elementary educa- tion was Joseph Lancaster, a Quaker, who founded what I were now known as British schools in the face of the ' most strenuous opposition of the clergy. Mr. amide, then dwelt at some length with the injustice done to the children, the teachers and the parents, and gave several illustrations in support of his contentions. In conclusion, he stated that they were accused of being mock-martyrs, fanatics and other complimentary names, but such accusations did not trouble a man who simply desired to obey the dictates of conscience. There was nothing to be ashamed of in civil disobedience. It was , the heroic resistance of law breakers that had won the liberties of the present day, and such a weapon was as old as human progress. He did not fear the result of this battle, they had not sought it, but they were glad that it had commenced. They would win far more than they had ever had before—full religious equality, and a truly national system of education freed for ever from I the fatal interference of the priest.

THE EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

A BRIEF MONTHLY MEETING. New Secondary School Head Master.

THE TRAINING OF PUPIL TEACHERS.

Less than half an hoursufficed to transactthe business at the Warrington Educhtion Committee's meeting on Monday evening. 'Mr. F. Monks, J.P., presided, and there were also present : Revs. Morley Stevenson, John Yonge and Father Wilson, Alderman Bennett, Alderman Pierpoint, Councillors Burton, Wilkinson, James Eyans, Dodds, Ellison, S. Davies, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, G. Walker, E. Plinston, J. D. Buckton, R. T. Fairclough, J. F. Smith, J. Upson, J. Bibby, and C. H. Unsworth, with J. Moore Murray (director of educa- tion), and J. Fairhurst (accountant). A PLEA AGAINST "EXTRAVAGANCE." HANDSOMELY BOUND VOLUMES OF "PROCEEDINGS."

The Finance Committee recommended the appoint- ment of Mr. Richard Price as committee and minute clerk (in place of Mr. E. Haigh promoted) at a com- mencing salary of 17s. 6d. per week. Accounts totalling £3,467 9s. 6d. were passed for payment. Mr. A. J. JOLLEY moved, and Councillor DODDS seconded the adoption of the minutes. The Rev. JOHN YONGE said there was one matter which, in the absence of Mr. May, he would like to call attention to. He was sure Mr. May would have done so if he had been present, because he was a very earnest opponent of all extravagance in matters of finance. He (Mr, Yonge) honed he would not be misunderstood or considered ungrateful to their friend the Director of Education for the very handsome present he recently sent to him and, he supposed, to every mem- ber of the committee in the shape of a very handsomely bound volume, emblazoned with the coat of arms of the borough, with gilt letters on the back. He con- sidered the volume to be a great ornament to his book- shelf, but he ventured to suggest that when they came to reflect, the minutes of the committee hardly deserved such a rich binding, and it was hardly necessary that they should be sent to the members in that form,seeing that all received copies every month and could keep them for reference. He believed the cort of the books bound in half-calf with gilt lettering would be about thirty or forty half crowns. Considering the outcry which had been raised so much lately against Corporation extravagance, he suggested that plain dloth binding would meet the requirements in the future. Alderman PIERPOINT remarked that the bound volumes of the Education Committee's proceedings were exact replicas of those issued by the Town Council. Rev.JoHN YONGE : That makes it so much worse. Alderman PIERPOINT said it was an advantage to have bound copies of the proceedings of the Council, as it enabled members to refer back for years when important questions arose. He did not consider it was extrava- gance to have the books, and he was sorry that Mr. Yonge thought the committee had been extravagant. The Rev. JOHN YONGE : I don't think it is the committee. Alderman PIERPOINT thought the committee was of sufficient importance to have well-bound volumes. The Rev. JOHN YONGE : I throw out the suggestion that plain clothe would suit the purpose equally well. Alderman PIERPOINT : The Town Council must get their's altered first. They have set the example, and we are entitled to follow. The minutes were confirmed.

THE NEW HEADMASTER FOR THE SECONDARY SCHOOL. INCREASE OF STAFF. THE TRAINING OF PUPIL TEACHERS.

At a meeting of the School Management Committee the committee confirmed the arrangements for the various evening classes to be held during the coming session. They were published in detail in last Saturday's "Examiner." A sub-committee presented a report on the work of the Pupil Teachers' Centre which appeared in last week's Examiner." At a meeting on July 13th, it was resolved, that the following teachers be. appointed to the Secondary school and Technical Institute at the salary mentioned in each case; the engagements to date from times to be hereafter fixed, and to be determinable, in the case of the head master, by three months', and in the cases of the assistant teachers, by one month's notice to expire on the last day of any calendar month, given by or to the Committee, viz :—(a) head master, Mr. Ernest S. A. Robson, £250 per annum; (b) assistant master, Mr. William Donaghy, £l2O per annum; (c) assistant mistresses, Miss Mary L. Mitchell, Miss Edith Wilkin- son, £llO per annum in each case. At this stage the Chairman read a telegram from the Rev. Morley Stevenson stating that he had missed a train at Wigan and would be late. Mr. UPSON moved the adoption of the minutes. Allud- ing to the decision to employ an additional assistant at the Secondary school, he pointed out that the Inspector told the committee that they must st,aff the school so that each teacher should not have more that twenty- five pupils. That would work out rather economically than otherwise, because only a certainnumber of pupils could be taken ,in the physics and chemistry rooms at one time, and if the teachers had classes of thirty or more, only part of each class could be taken at once If each teacher had Oventy-five pupils, it would enable him or her to teach the whole of the class at one time. The Inspector also intimated in very broad terms that unless the appointment was made he, as representative of the Board of Education, would make the punishment fit the crime, and recommend a lower grant. A head- master had now been appointed in place of Mr. Gow. Mr. Robson was one of the selected candidates for the post twelve months ago when Mr. Gow received the appointment. On the present occasion he was selected from nearly a hundred applicants, and in the opinion of the committee he was the most suitable man.

Mr. JOLLEY seconded. The Rev. Moßim' STEVENSON had now arrived and gave a full explanation of the minutes. With regard to the discontinuance of the Parochial Evening Schools he pointed out that the great object of the com- mittee was to plant evening schools all over the town, so that there might be a school conveni- ent for each district. It was of no use to have them too thickly together, and at the end of the town where the Parochial schools were situated there was, perhaps, an over-abundance of evening schools. The consequence was that the attendances were not as good as they could wish, and it was felt that the schools with the smallest attendance should be discontinued. The com- mittee were making an effort not only to teach such subjects as would be thoroughly useful in the evening schools, but also to introduce occasionally lectures upon subjects of interest, so as to entice scholars to come, as well as to instruct them. Alluding to the warning to teachers in regard to the marking of the registers at the 'evening classes, the rev, gentleman said they all knew how extremely important it was that the registers should be accurately marked and a very large fine in the grant was the outcome or penalty of any neglect. He was sorry to say that during the last session there were certain irregularities connected with the registration. He did not for a moment impute anything except just a little heedlessness, but the matter was so important that the co-nmittee felt it their duty to call attention to it rather strongly. With regard to the Secondary school, it might seem to some members that the increase of staff was making very considerable strides, but he would like to point out that the matter did not rest with the committee but with the regulations. The inspector made it very clear that he would not sanction a larger class in a school of that character than twenty- five for a teacher. Whether that was reasonable or not was a matter of opinion, perhaps, but it was in accord- ance with the regulations, and if they did not comply with them they were liable to lose the grants. The committee would see that the application for increase of staff from the managersnf the Ladies' School of Industry was not acceded to because the staff was up to the requirements which the committee had laid down. The staffing of the schools was a very difficult question which, he believed, they would have to apply themselves tr, in the future, but at the present time the committee were going upon the plan of trying to staff every school in the town up to a certain standard before they made any further increase. With reference to the pupil teachers' centre, be was sure they would all feel very glad that the new instructions had swept away the old form of secondary school, so that they did not speak now of a school a or b, and at the same time they had introduced a larger amount of direct English instruction into the curriculum and a, smaller amount of scientific instruction. The conimit.r tee did not of course, undervalue the scientific instruc- tion for a moment, but there was no doubt,he thought in the opinion of most good judges, that the amount of time given to scientific instruction under the old regulations was excessive, and it was impossible to give that grounding in English subjects which was so extremely necessary for those who were to be the teachers of the future. The inspec- tors were very strong indeed in expressing their opin- ion that the pupil teachers should receive their school practice in the very best schools.and of course the com- mittee would all see that if a boy or a girl was going to be a teacher,it was essential that he or she should be at once got into the best methods. It would be an in- vidious task for the committee to have to say which were the best schools, so they had asked the inspectors to name those schools which they preferred the pupil teachers to be placed in. That would con- siderably add to the work of the head teachers in the schools, and make their office even more responsible than it was now. In the opinion of the committee the work should be recognized by some small addition to their income. Mr. Upson had, he understood, alluded to the appointment of Mr. Robson as headmaster of the Secondary school and Technical Institute, and he had no doubt he had done so in terms of high commenda- tion, and in that belief he (Mr. Stevenson) would like to support all he had said. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Robson was second for the post last year, and he thought the

committee could not do better than welcome him in Mr. Gow's place. The minutes were approved.

THE OVERCROWDED STATE OF THE SCHOOLS. At a meeting of the School Attendance Committee, a letter, dated the 14th June, from the managers of the Hamilton-street National school, asking, in reply to the letter sent to them in accordance with the decision of the committee at their last meeting with reference to the overcrowded state of that school, for an inter- view with the committee, was submitted, together with a letter, dated the 27th June, from the Board of Educa- tion, enquiring whether the committee are yet in a position to forward to the Board their proposals with a view to relieving the pressure upon the accommoda- tion of this school. A copy of the communication sent to the Board of Education, in reply to this last mentioded letter, was also submitted, and it was decided that a copy of this letter be sent to the managers of the Hamilton-street school, with an intimation, that, before doing anything further in the matter, the committee propose to amait the reply of the Board. Councillor EmasoN, in moving the confirmation of the minutes, said he believed the difficulty of over- crowding at Hamilton-street school had been temporarily overcome. The Board of Education had written stating that they would recognise the school this year on the understanding that steps were taken to meet the diffi- culty of overcrowding. Councillor DAVIES seconded. The minutes were passed without further comment. THE PROPOSED NEW SCHOOLS.

The Sites and Buildings Committee recommended that the following tenders for the painting and decora- ting of the undermentioned schools be accepted, viz : St. Ann's National, Messrs. Allen and Sons £3O; St. Peter's (John street,)lVlessrs. W. J. Shaw and Sons, £9; St. Mary's, Latchford, Mr. W. Winstanley, £l2 155.; Warrington Parochial (boys), Mr. George Hoult, £2O; Warrington Parochial (Infants), Mr. W. Winstanlay £ll 15s. 6d.; Wycliffe British, Mr. W. Winstanley £32 16s. 6d. _ _

Mr. R. T. FAIRCLOUGH moved the confirmation of the minutes. In referring to the tenders for painting and decorating ha said the work was now in hand and would be completed during the holidays. Councillor JAMES EVANS seconded. . _

Mr. PLINSTON asked for information with reference to the building of new schools. He thought it was a matter on which they might have a little more informa- tion. Almost all the schools were overcrowded and there had been a good deal of sickness among children. He believed much of the sickness was due to lack of proper accommodation. Resolutions had been sent to the Sites and Buildings Committee by the School Attend- ance Committee, but no information had been forthcom- ing as to what was being done to meet the difficulty. Councillor BURTON inquired whether the lowest tenders had been accepted in all cases for painting. Mr. FAIRCLOUGH replied in the affirmative. In answer to Mr. Plinston's question, Mr. Fairclough said the committee had inspected seven or eight different sites. At the last meeting of the committee it was decided to purchase two sites. It was rather a delicate matter to discuss at present, and he did not propose to say anything further, because it might tie the hands of the committee in purchasing land. He might say, how- ever, that the committee were quite alive to the fact that new schools were wanted and they were trying to purchase the land in an economical manner. Mr. PLINSTON said he was perfectly satisfied with the explanation. The minutes were then passed. FOUNDATION MANAGERS APPOINTED.

At a meeting of the General Purposes Committee, notifications of the following foundation managers under the final orders were made namely :—Heathside Schools, the Rev. W. Braceeamp, and Messrs. L. W. Reynolds, A. J. Jolley and J. H. Smethurst; Latchford National Schools, the Rev. G. C. Briggs, and Messrs. J. Bernard, John Fairclough and James Lowe; Sacred Heart Schools, tbe Revs. M. Ryan, J. McGrath and A. D'Arcy, and Mr. John Gibbins ; St. Peter's, the Rev. B. Percival, Coun- cillor Roberts, and Messrs. J.Bamforth and Peter Edgar. —lt was reported that owing to the death of Mr. James Bolton, Mr. William Eckersley, of Latchford, had been appointed a foundation manager of the St. James' schools.—lt was resolved that Mr. George Walker be appointed a representative manager in the place of Mr. J. H. Fisher, resigned, for the Warrington Parochial School.—A report of the special sub-committee appointed with reference to the transfer of St. George's Mission and the Latchfora National Schools was submitted and approved. On the motion of the CHAIRMAN, seconded by Mr PLINsToN, the minutes were approved and the com- mittee rose.

===== 0002601_19040723_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
SANKEY PARISH COUNCIL.

The Pollution of Sankey Brook.

A meeting of the Great Sankey Parish Council was held on Monday evening. Mr. G. Thompson presided, and there were also present :--Mr. G. Higgs, Mr. W. Taylor, Mr. W. Furness, Mr. H. Fairclough, Mr. F. T. Jefferson. with the; clerk, (Mr. Smith) .

===== 0002601_19040723_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
WATER SUPPLY EXTENSION.

The Chairman said at the last meeting of the Rural District Council he proposed a resolution urging. the necessity of improving the water supply to Mr. Taylor's farm. The work was taken in hand at once by the Cor- poration, and he thought they would all admit that it had been done very promptly. Mr. Taylor was very well satisfied with the work that had been done.

===== 0002601_19040723_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
THE REPAIRING OF BUTTERMILK BRIDGE.

The Clerk said he had received a letter from Mr. Higgs, stating that he desired to call attention to the bridge over Sankey Brook, which was in a very bad state of repair. Mr. Higgs said since he wrote the letter the bridge had been temporarily repaired. He said temporarily because the bridge had not been repaired sufficiently substantial. The least the Rural District Council could do would be to repair their portion of the bridge equally as well as thelCorporation had done their part (Hear, hear). the bridge was certainly in a very dangerous condition, and a child from 6to 10 years of age could very easily have tumbled into the brook and been drowned. He was not at all satisfied with the way the Rural Council had repaired their portion of the bridge. The Chairman said he called the attention of the Dis- trict Council to the matter at the last meeting and stated that the repair of the bridge had not been done to his satisfactionor the satisfaction of the people in theneighbourhood. The Surveyor said the work had not been finished He (the chairman) was nearly tripped on the bridge. Mr. Higgs said the rail had been put right but the pathway of the bridge was in need of repair. If a man caught his foot in the hoop-iron he would be thrown on his face. Some of the steps had been worn away and required replacing. The Chairman observed that when he called attention to the matter at the Rural District Council meeting, the Chairman of the Main Roads Committee said they would try to arrange with the Corporation to have an iron bridge put down in place of the present one. There was no doubt the state of the bridge was a very serious matter' and if anything happened, the District Council would be liable. However, he thought they could take it for granted that the matter would be attended to and that there would be no further cause of complaint. Mr. Fairclough : The bridge wants a new bottom putting in. That is unless they decide to put down a new bridge. The Clerk thought that neither the Warrington Cor- poration nor the District Council knew how much the bridge was used. At least 1,000 people went over it every, Sunday night. And now the school children were on holiday it would be heavier still. Mr. HIGGS said it was the fact of the children being on holiday that made him anxious about the matter. The bridge was a disgrace to the district. He proposed that the Clerk write to the District Council saying they were pleased to hear that they were about to approach the Corporation with the idea of having an iron Bridge put down, as the present one was so 'danger- ous that it was absolutely necessary that something should be done.

Mr. Furness seconded the proposition, and it was carried.

THE POLLUTION OF SANKEY BROOK.

ARE THE CORPORATION THE CHIEF OFFENDERS? Mr. JEFFERSON remarked that people in the neigh- bourhood were complaining very much about the stench from Sankey Brook that was emitted both morning and evening. Those who had to pass over the bridge as often as he did would find that the brook was in an abominable state, and something ought to be done to purify it. The least they could do was to write to the Disttict Council and ask them to take the matter up. He would move a resolution to that effect, as he thought it was getting a perfect nuisance. After the recent thunderstorm, the stench was abominable. The CHAIRMAN said he also brought this question before the District Council and told them that the stench was very bad indeed. The other members of the Council then took the matter up,and Messrs. Mortimer and Company, tanners, who appeared to be the greatest offenders, were served with a notice to abate the nuis- ance. Mr. Furnesg thought the Warrington Corporation were by far the chief offenders. He did not know whether it was common knowledge that the secrage from the Winwickend of Warrington was turned into the brook practically crude, and it was that—neutralized slightly by the matters turned into the brook from the gas works—which caused the offence as much as any- thing else. The whole of the sewage from the Winwick end, from Watkin-street was turned into the brook. The Clerk did not think it was common knowledge.

Mr. Jefferson said furtherimildine operations were being carried on in Orford Avenue and thesewage would be sent in the brook. If something was not done it would get worse. Mr. Furness said he would second the resolution. He was of the opinion that the Corporation were the worst offenders, and it was within memory that the father of the present Medical Officer had called the attention of the Health Committee to it time after time, and told them that something ought to be done to minimize the offensive character of tke effluvia turned into, the brook. He (Mr. Furness) would move that a deputation should in-

Authors of letters intended for publication must mall oases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on one side onlg of the paper andisent in, if possible,not later than Thursday.

Howley Ward Vacancy. Sir,-1 have much pleasure in supporting the candi- dature of Arr. Jacob Sankey in the Howley Ward. As one who desires to see party politics eliminated from municipal affairs, I regret that he has consented to con- test the election as the nominee of a political party. However, he has assured me that he is opposed to every form of illegal and immoral trading, that he is a deter- mined advocate of economy, and that he is prepared to aupport every proposal which tends to promote the physical and moral well-being of the community. The qualifications of his opponentare unknown to me, except in so far that he is a member of a" trade "already represented in the public life .of our town, out of all proportion to its legitimate claims,and a "trade" which openly and avowedly asserts that all other interests are subordinate and subservient to its own. For these reasons amongst many others I appeal to all who have the well-being of our tovvnat heart to support by every legitimate means the candidature of Mr. Sankey.—Sincerely yours, HERBERT DIDSICICO.

Baptist Church, Warrington. - The Rev. Herbert Dunnico's Mode of

Controversy.

Sir,—Miss Marie Corelli writing in the ' Bystander ', has been attacking "the dignitaries of the Church," and one phrase in her article would, I think, be of great use to the Rev. Herbert Duanico, if he would only read it. "They (the clergy) are chosen out and solemnly ordained to be the friends,• lovers and guides of humanity—rot to be selfish pedants, quarrelsome quidnuncs, and bigoted despots." Of the Rev. Herbert Dunnico's modes of controirersy very little can be-said to the writer's credit. I have, think, read .neaily all his letters on the many different subjects which he thinks it his duty to write upon, and very rare are ths exceptions in which he has written in a Christ4ike spirit. In his letter of Saturday last on the Social Problemhe talks of" lamentable ignorance," "narrow-minded, inconsistent and incorrect statements" in a manner that would lead people to believe that he was the only person who could possibly be right. A friend of mine once entered into what he intended to be a friendly controversy with the Rev. Herbert Dunnico on.the Education Question and just to show the unchristianlike manner in which the rev, gentleman conducts a controversy I will give just a few phrases. Just notice the Ohristianlike sarcasm.

" The letter certainly reflects great credit on the writer and his knowledge of scripture (sic) ought to have obtained for him holy orders ere this." '" It would bel an irreparable 'loss to the com- munity that such a genius (sic) should be left to blush unseen and waste his sweetness (sic) on the desert air."

Please observe the (sic) dear reader. My opinion of Mr. Donnie%) is (I might be wrong I hope I am) that he wants to make a name for himself. He must get to the front.He will be anything—a Liberal a Socialist, a Passive Resister, a law breaker in fact anything. Mr. Dunnico, take my advice, drop it all. never milid the political situation foiin this irreligious age the policy of neither side will fit in with the sermon on the Mount. Yours Scc. A. A.

Stockton Heath V. Warrington.

The Widening of Old London-road, &c. Sir,—As I anticipated, Mr. Westbrook on making careful investigation found that he was in error in stat- ing in his first letter that the width of Old London-road was to be 50 feet wide, to the extent of at least one- third in the length of it. With regard to the width of 50 feet near the Mulberry Tree he is quite correct, but in mentioning the width of 55 feet he omitted to say that the road opposite the Red Lion was practically that width before any negotiations were entered into at all. His first contention therefore to quote his own letter "That the Parish Council obtained terms so far as regards road width gave the Stockton Heath people a road 50 feet wide instead of one 45 feet wide" falls to the ground. Mr. Westbrook I also notice quotes legal authorities in support of his contention as to the rights of the Parish Council in the matter, but I hope he will excuse me from looking up law reports and books to see whether there are not opinions to the contrary. Life is altogether too short to do that. For my own part I am quite content to accept the Town Clerk's opinion 'On the whole subject viz. 'That a Parish Council has no locas stand; under the Light Railway Acts and also that the arrangements for the widening of Old London-road were made with the County Council." I need only say that the adoption of a policy of " sweet reasonableness" will be found to pay Stockton Heath best in the long run. So far the latter policy has only resulted in checking enterprises and busi- nesses, &c., which would be of benefit to the district— though they will come despite all opposition,—viz., the light railways, the electric light new schools, etc. Unfortunately, however, that is not the only feature in the matter, for I hear well grounded rumours of the adoption of a retaliatory policy on the part of Warring- ton in the shape of increased tram fares, the closing of their schools to the children of Stockton Heath, ant also the stoppage of the open market near the Mulberry I Tree. As have stated before, and do so again, Stock- ton Heath is not yet in such a position as she can afford to throw the gauntlet down to Warrington. It is a suicidal policy.—Yours faithfully, E. ENGLAND.

The Representation of Warrington. Mr. A.H. Crosfield and the Labour Party.

---Sir,—The question put to rue at the Liberal meeting held yesterday as to who is to be candidate for the Progressive party is one which, for reasons which I am sure your _readers will understand, I should prefer to avoid discussing. But perhaps it is as well to say a few words about it, because the very fact of this question being addressed to me may lead to some misapprehen- sion on the matter. May -I therefore just remind our friend who put it, and perhaps others of your readers, tlist the choice of a candidate for the Progressive party rests, and always has rested, with that party itself expressing its will through a General Council and the Executive Committee selected by that Council. I feel sure that my friends on the Executive, and many other responsible members of the Liberal party will endorse what I said last night as to my not having tried to thrust myself forward as candidate. I did not do so at the last election, nor when last year the question of a prospective candidate was udder consideration; I simply left my- self in the hands of the Executive. I had many and long discussions at the time with our friends as to the advisability of adoptinga Labour candidate, and it was only in deference to the opinions they then very emphat- ically expressed that I consented to take the very honourable and also responsible position of prospective candidate. As things stand I intend to be guided by the advice of those who after all fpresent nineteen twentieths of the progressive forces in Warrington, if not a good deal more, and I shall certainly not fail my friends at a critical time, or be deterred in the work that lies before us next autumn and winter at the bid- ding of a mere handful of irreconcilables. I have never at any time exerted myself to prevent the adoption of a first rate Labour candidate, such a man for instance as Mr. Burt or Mr. Fenwick, or Mr. Broadhurst, or Mr. Crooks, men of that sort whose principles and sound judgment would unite the Progres- sive forces in the town in a way that no extreme Socialist could possibly unite them. On the other hand since the Liberal Executive did me the great honour to bring forward my name as Progres- sive candidate, I may say during discussions with many of the leaders of the great trade organisations in the town, I have been happy to find that they approved of the selection, and I may add that if I did not feel that one had. the confidence and support both of these gentle- men and of those they represent, I would not for one moment think of consenting to fill the position of pros- pective candidate. But it is a very deep gratification to me to know from them that I have a large measure of their confidence and support, and given that, Liberal friends of mine may be very sure that I shall not flinch and that when the time comes I shall be willing and proud to accept the position as candidate and make the best fight I can. At that time I hope we shall find that all members of the Progressive party will display, as Mr. Howes urged both last night and on Tuesday night "unity and enthusiasm." If unfortunately a small minority is inclined to fly off at a tangent, and instead of working for what is practical and feasible spend their time in looking for a short cut to Utopia, may I respectfully remind diem that it has long been found a bad policy to drop the bone for the shadow, and, to quote another homely adage, that the vast majority of Progressives, both of the working class and of the middle class in Warrington have not the slightest inten- tion of allowing "The tail to wag the dog."—Yours _ A. H. CROSFIELD.

Barremman, Hoylake, July 21st, 1904. P.S. Of course I use the word "Socialist" in a very different sense to that in which Sir William Harcourt used it a few years since when in a half jocular way he said that nowadays we are all Socialists. The distinc- tion is obvious.
