The Social Problem.

Sir,—Again I ask for your kind indulgence for a short space to reply to Mr. Dunnico. Firstly, I wish to ask wherein I show an arrogant and flippant manner? Is it arrogance then for a citizen to use the liberty open to all, to ask an honest minister, straightforward questions through an honest newspaper? And which questions he very honestly evades, and in place of an answer throws out a few Christian-like epithets, as well becomes a Christian minister. And as to flippancy, that is untrue. I have too deep a sense of the sacredness of our cause,

Application for Discharge. Before Deputy-Judge Cuthbert Smith, at the War. rington County Court on Thursday, Mr. Madden,barrister (instructed by Mr. Arthur Browne), applied on behalf of Mr. H. R. Ezard, formerly a builder and carrying on business in Padgate and district, for his discharge from bankruptcy. Mr. Dibb (official receiver) presented a report on the applicant's bankruptcy. The receiving order was made against Mr. Ezard, on his own petition, the Official Receiver said, in July 1899. The unsecured liabilities were £4,399, but his total liabilities were £27,000. The assets to meet the unsecured liabilities were expected to produce £2,023, but as a matter of fact they only realised £42 3s. 4d., the difference between the two amounts Wit.' accounted for by the bankrupt having over-estimated the various property equities. No dividend was, paid to the unsecured creditors. In commenting upon Mr. Ezard's bankruptcy, the Official Receiver said the bankrupt had been guilty of " rash and hazardous" speculation. Starting with £5O capital, he built in the 'course of a few years. 198 houses involving a liability of £27,000. He did not keep proper books of account and in one speculation in land at Ellesmere he spent £5OO in filling up before he was able to use it. Mr. Madden said after alapse of five years he appeared for the discharge of the bankrupt. He would dismiss from his mind the adjectives which the Official Re- ceiver had used in his report in which he spoke of "wil- ful Ignorance and extravagantand wild speculation." Mr. Dibb: lam only using the words of the Act of Parliament. Mr. Madden: Therefore they have no significance. (Laughter.) His Honour : The words used are "rash and hazard- ous "speculation. Mr. Madden said he was not complaining. The bank- rupt was the sonnf a builder who made some money and retired. The applicant had been with Johnson's, of Liverpool for about five years when he started business on his own account. He had very little money of his own. Whilst doing some work at Pad.gate he met with a man who prompted him to go into these building speculations, and it was the refusal of this man to advance him £BOO that caused him to be- come bankrupt at the last moment. As far as the bank- rupt was concerned,he was led into building speculations Thevall knew perfectly well what the game was in re- gard to buildings. The question was whether the bankrupt in this case was a fit and proper person to recommence business. He was led into these building transactions by Mr. Henry Thornton. He did not know the man. He originally c,atne to build two housesat Pad. gate and while there he met Mr. Thornton, who appeared as a mortgagee for £12,090. Mr. Thornton said "I will litulvou all tha mance/ vaa wiat." In this ,vty Mr. Ezard built something like 126 houses. Under the impression that he was to receive a further loan of £BOO, Mr. Ezard signed a deed at the offices of Mr. Thornton's solicitors, but instead of obtaining the money he received a letter stating that it was a collateral security. Mr. Ezard, continued Mr. Madden, should have taken steps to have set aside the deed. The trustee of the estate thought something of the kind ought to have been done, and the opinion of counsel was taken as to setting the deed aside. His Honour said counsel's opinion was against taking any action. Mr. Madden said it was then too late to take action. Proceeding, he went into details as to the transaction, and said Mr. E6trd ty ta prepared to go into the !sox aryl swear as to the correctness of his statement. Ceunsel proceeded to oontend that there was a large equity in the properties erected which the mortgagees had got the benefit of. Mortgagees never lent the full amount of the property and Mr. Ezard estimated that there was an equity of £2.009 which kad gone to the mortgagees. If the prepertiea had beer' sold and realised their value, ha clat?niel there would have been a large margin to this estate. Was this man entitled to a discharge? He would ask his honortr to look elm the position of a speculative builder as being different from that of a man engaged ma commer- cial undertaking or in a .1:Ion business. In the two last named businesses, the man was not so much the victim of those who were engaged in the swim with him. Most, men would admit that building was a hazardous game, In this ease the mortgagees had got their money. The Oficial Receiver: All the mortgagees have not got their money. His flonortr: Presnming they have got their money, I don't see how it affects the question. Mr. Madden said there was no suggestion that the bankrupt was not a hard-working man or that he lived extravagantly. The Oficial Receiver stated in his report that the debtor gave every assistance he possibly could. He (Mr. Madden) therefore asked with some confidence that Mr. Ezard might be granted his discharge. Mr. Dibb said he could not answer Mr. IVladden's observations.No doubt Mr. Madden had said what he had been instructed to say, but he (Mr. Dibb) did not wish to unduly press the matter against the bankrupt. He did not say that the bankrupt had defrauded ma serious sense at all. With regard to the term "rash and hazardous" speculation. when they considered that the bankrupt had no means, it was a most injudicious thing for him to embark in these large speculations without seeing that he was protecting his creditors as he ought to have done. Even if the application were granted, there must, be a suspension for two years. It was to the debtor's credit that he had waited five years before coming to the Court and asking for his discharge. His Honour said he was sure Mr. Madden arrl his client wonld be glad to hear the remarks of the Oficial Receiver because he (His Honour) thought Mr. Dibb had put the matter very. fairly. But assuming that the bankrupt could prove every word Mr. Madden had said,he did not think it offered very much excise. it was the old story; of the Garden of Eden: someone else told me to do it and I did it. It was the way In which he did it, that a judge'had to look at it in deal- ing with the bankruptcy laws. When one considered that a man with £5O caaital started on building speculations and erected 198 houses incurring a liability of £27,000, anyone who said that it was not rash and that it was not hazardous, seemed to him to be saying that which could not be substantiated. The important thing in this case was that after he was led into this speculative building he did not keep proper accounts, and the unfortunate result must follow. There ' was certainly reckless speculation in spending £5OO In tilling up land at Ellesmere before he could use it for buildine purposes. He found that the offences set out by the Official Receiver were proved. But in view of the fact that the bankrupt had waited five years be- fore making that application, he thought he would meet the circumstances of the case, especially after what the Official Receiver had said, by granting the bankrupt his discharge,after a suspension of three years.

Mr. CROSFIELD, who was loudlylcheered on rising to speak, said it was his privilege that evening, to address he thought for the first time an open-air Meeting,at any rite in that part of the town. It was a little difficult for anyone to address a political meeting at the present time, because as he heard Mr. Morley say the other night, they, as a party, had rather too much stock-in- trade. (Hear, hear and laughter.) The enormities of the Government—and he said it deliberately, and not merely as a platform gibe—had been so great that one scarcely knew where to make a start. What they had seen during the last few years, perhaps, illustrated better than anything he had known, a familiar saying that they used to hear from their old friend Mr. IVlcMinnies,that " if you give a Tory Government rope enough and time enough, they are sure to hang them- selves." (Laughterand cheers.) And certainly the ex- perience of the last few years had been so bad and had so disgusted the majority of responsible sensible citizens that he thought the old saying of Mr. McMinnies came home forcibly to them. It rather re- minded him of a story of Edwin Waugh's, which, said thespeaker, he had no doubt was Familiar to most of those present. It Was about a couple who went from Lancashire for a holiday—a sea trip to Ireland. The weather happened to be rather rough, and he (the speaker) dared say they knew what excursion steamers were like when the weather happened to be bad. After a couple of hours, the couple became very seedy. The old lady was on deck and her good husband was below, and she sent word that if her husband wanted to see her in this world he had better come and come quickly. The old fellow happened to be rather worse than his wife and he sent word back "Nah, nab. Ize not coming. She's had her own road all her life and she shall have it now, and if hoo is for deeing boo must dee, for ize not be long after her." (Loud laughter.) It was di ficult to make a wise selection of -the subjects to discuss, but between now and the great fight before them they intended to have everyone of these serious questions thrashed out, not by himself but by other public speakers not the least of whom was on the platform that evening. (Cheers.) On the following evening he hoped to be able to say something about the foreign policy because the Tory member for Warrington had laid great stress on the fact that foreign affairs at any rate were better conducted under a Conservative Government than under a Liberal Government. (A Voice: "Bosh.") That night, however, he would say nothing about foreign affairs, but very briefly recapitulate the main questions that they had to consider as they affected the home- steads in England. There was one question which was inextricably mixed up with the foreign conduct of affairs, and which bore upon labour iluestions at home. He had spent a great dell of time in investigating the whole of THE paocEEDrNGSIx SOITTIf A.F RTC %.

before, during and since the war, and he did not sup- pose there was any question which had exercised the minds of artisans of Warrin.gton and England more than the abominable re-actionary attempt to introduce Chinese labour into South Africa. That had a bearing on the question of the foreign policy, because he was sure they would all recollect only too well how they were asked not merely to support the war when it was commenced, but to encourage the commencement of a war which was to be a miner's war and to establish a great white man's country in the Transvaal and the Free State. in that connection the matter had a bear- ing on the foreign policy, but from a simple point of view everyone could understand that if they were going to have a large amount of cheap Chinese labour• introduced into the country, where with proper conditions and pay they could get plenty of labour on the spot, if they did that they were either going to have the white men superseded or else his standard of life dragged down to the conditions which obtained when Chinese labour was in force. That was a question which most deeply exercisedithe minds of all respectable artisans, whether in Sonth Africa or in England. (Ileac, hear.) Another question which had greatly exercised the minds of

TRADES UNIONISTS

all through the country. and noon which he had had much discussion with Trades Unionists, was the pure freedom. which Trade Unionists had to unite and com- bine in defence of their legitimate rights—a matter upon which he felt very strongly. He had had some experience of combination, and in his case innocent combination amongst capitalists, because it was now 16 years since he was voted into the secretaryship of an association of employers of labour, and which was not an unimportant association in this country. They had united to prevent the fierceness of competition driving prices down below cost. It would be very intricate for him to explain the work of the association and also out of place. The association had operate 4 for the benefit of the whole trade, and as it had prevented undue com- petition and the squeezing out of some of the weaker firms it had operated for the benefit of the workingmen themselves. (Hear, hear.) Did they think that holding those views he would stand before diem claiming for the class to which he belonged rights which he did not think ought to be conceded to working men in the same way before the law? (Cheers.) That was a very simple issue. They had some complicated issues to deal with, but that was not one of them. It was asimple proposi- tion, and he thought every one of them must be annoyed that he vote of Warrington the other day was cast against the principle for which Mr. Shackleton had con- tended in the Bill. He did not believe there was a working man present who more thoroughly felt than he did the justice and necessity of conceding and safe-guard- ing the same rights for the working class in that respect which were enjoyed by the employers of labour. It was a matter of justice,and he assured his Conservative friends it was one of the things which tended to thebeace and good government of this country in the long run. (Hear hear.) All his life he had been taught to take a pride n seeing the gradual and orderly progre3s of the work-, trig classes and the gradual raising of the standard of life which they saw in England, and, he was thankful to say, better in this country than in any other country in the world. That, with progressive Liberals, he held to be a matter of paramount importance. (Cheers.) There was nothing in the country so important unless it were the wiping out of, perhaps, the deepest blot on the civilization of modern ti nes, a blot that was described as the submerged tenth, of the three or four miliions of people who were in the lowest social scale. He had argued at many meetings in siVarrington that before they talked about dealing thoroughly and radically with the BETTER. HOUSING OF TFIF, PEOPLE

They might do something at housing without it, but they would only be tinkering, and that brought another question to his mind. Whether in the years to come, when this wretched Tory reaction had ceased, and when they went in for a long sell of orogressive govern- ment. whether they had to deal with the critical ques- 'tion of the land laws or tried to repeal an abominable piece of legislation being forced through the House now against the interests of the whole of the people, whether they dealt with the licensing question or the land question, they would most assuredly be brought face to face with thatl solid, dill, selfish opposition they had to meet with, whenever a Liberal Govern- ment came into pqw3r. and then their work was either emasculated or destroyed by the House of Lords. is., the endeavour toameliorate the suffering of work- ,g humanity ever to deserve such a frivolous reproach. Dunnico also accuses me of being illogical. Well, I have never studied the hard and fast rules of logic it Is true—although somewhat fond of the science—so that a lesson front a University student in the game would be a treat I would not miss on any account, but he must show my illogic in its bearings on my first letter, and real contention which was: That having publicly, in the Theatre, and Co-operative Hall, etc., declared ' himself to be a Socialist, why do we afterwards find him strenuously working in the Liberal cause? I ask again, is it possible to be a true Socialist and true Liberal at the same time? Is it logical? (And then to throw the stone of inconsistency at another). In reply to the questions Mr. Dunnico shirks the point and goes off .to a eulogy on Christian teaching—saying that I omitted the word Christian. Well, if a sample of Mr. Dunnico's good logic consists in his imagining it possible for people to consider a Christian minister in the light of a non-Christian Socialist then give me my ignorance and illogic which pronounce such an impossibility. Does Mr. Dunnico infer that he considers Socialists as non-Christian ? a point I referred to last week but which he ingeniously ' slides over. If so he is greatly mistaken, and in spite of his logic it does not follow that because a man is a Socialist he must necessarily be a non-Christian,any more than it follows that a non-thfistian must be a Socialist. I wonder if Mr. Dunnico is helping the aims of the non- Christians in the Liberal party? Analysed—what an illogical position. Oh yes, there are Christians amongst the Socialists. Only the other day one of the highest ladies of our land—a Christian, and earnest Socialist— Lady Warwick, was asked ifshe would become a member of the British Institute of Social Service with which this same Social Service League is affiliated, but she courteously refused owing to the demands upon her time by other duties. And these are her own words "In joining with those who, with a self-sacrifice,Christian in the highest sense are working to build up the great labour movement which is slowly but surely bringing a message of hope to the victims of an unjust social system." Perhaps now Mr. Dunnico will believe that there are Christians amongst the Socialists. One more point, then I will trespass no longer. In last week's issue Mr. Dunnico accuses me of inconsistency. He may take my good wishes how he likes, but that as a reproach from hint is rich. Thanks, I do not fit the cap on, sir, but leave it to be worn by members of the Warrington Liberal Executive Committee, who pander for popularity to Socialist audiences. Will Mr. Dunnico now oblige with a bit more of his hard hitting—lam not very much afraid —and tell me wherein lies my bad logic, narrowminded- ness and above all" ignorance of the principles of the party of which I profess to be a member." A lefinition of Socialism or of the principles of the Labour party, by a Liberal opponent will be interesting and of immense value to the party at this very critical time for then we shall be able

"Tae see oorsels as ithers see us, It will free mony a blunder free as _ _ _ _

And foolish notion." As Mr. Dunnico has taken the initiative in hurling accusations perhaps he will not mind my following his noble example. I have proved him to beillogical. I have proved him to be inconsistent anti, since his Socialism is only fit for the few" who profess and call themselves Christians," perhaps also I have proved him nazrowminded. Our Socialism goes beyond that. It is just as broad as the earth, as great as the human family, and its foundations are justice and equity.— Very sincerely, P. B.

They would have to face the question of the House of Lords, and when they came to fight their battle he hoped it was not going to be an ephemeral bit of enthusiasm. They would do no good with a couple of years of progressive legislation. They needed a general union of all the progressive forces and, as Sir John Brunner said to him the other day, 'one thing this country wants now is about 20 ,years of solid strong Radical Clovern- ment." (Cheers,. Some of his friends told him (Mr. Crostield) that he was a bit knagwinded,and he admitted that in Warrington a politician ought to be chary about that. In connection with that he might tell them a story of a man well-known in Warrington,a man of great ability, whose son was also a man of great ability. He heartily wishedhe was on their side. He was sure he would not mind him telling the story about his father. It was a little incident that happened in the House, and they knew that however clever, able, and eloquent a man might be the members were inclined to poke fun at him sometimes. This happened in the early seventies when, as they knew, after the great Midlothian campaign the Liberals had their knives into" Dizzy," and in the House of Commons "Dizzy" was attacked very severely and as he thought very deservedly. On one occasion, -the late Mr. Peter Rylands gave Disraeli a very .warm time of it, but he thought he went on a little bit too long, and he was not sure that he did not interfere with the dinner hour. (Laughter). The members did not like that, and so when Mr. Rylands had exceeded the limit a slip of paper was passed down the front Opposi- tion Bench and this was _ what was on it.—

Preposterous Peter, prithee cut it short; That" Dizzy" doeth what he didn't ouht We know, but still, life would be sweeter With ten Disraeli's than one Peter ! (Loud laughter and cheers.) The Rev. HERBERT DowNrco, who arrived later, took the chair and addressed the meeting. He apologised for his late arrival, and proceeded to express the hope that the workingmen of Warrington would not degrade their manhood and disgrace their town and blot the lives of their wives and children by selling their votes for a pint of beer. (Hear, hear.) If he were contesting the Borough he should describe himself as a labour candi- date, not a labour representation candidate—because the Labour Representation Committee's candidates were narrower than some Liberals. He preferred men of the class of John Burns, Will Crooks and Shackleton. (Applause.) Who were the Labour Representation Committee in Warrington? It could be proved that they did not represent one-tenth of the labour in the town. Whom, then, did they represent? They repre- sented nobody adequately, except the Independent Labour Party. Let them have a Labour Representation Committee by all means. He believed in it and he would be a member of it, but if they were to have a Labour Representation Committee, then in all common sense and all goodness let it be a Labour Representa- tion Committee representative of labour. (Hear, hear.) There were many great questions now in the public mind. First, the iniquitous Education Act. Then there was Protection ; not Mr. Chamberlain's Protection, but the protection of the working man. (Hear, hear. That must be attended to. (Hear, hear.) The land question was another highly important matter and was at the root of almost all other 'questions, and in addition there was the question of preferential tariffs, which he hoped would receive its death knell at the next election. (Cheers.) He had read Mr. Crosfield's speeches, and there was not a single matter affecting the general welfare of the workingmari within the limit of practical politics, that he was not as sound upon as the most pronounced Labour man or Radical in the House of Commons. Therefore if the workingmen of the country would only consider these great questions in the light of civic righteousness instead of as Tories, Liberals or Labour men, then he would have no fear of the result. (Cheers.) Mr. Crosfield was their captain and they asked him to take a broad and firm stand on every progressive question. They did not wish him to pander to his friends or hi l foes, because for every man who ran away because of that, six men would join him. (Hear,hear.)Whether Mr.Crosfleld won or lost the election he would then have the glad consciousness of knowing that he had fought an honourable battle and struck a blow in defence of those principles which alone could make a nation great. (Cheers.) THE GOVERNMENT'S MISMANAGEMENT. MR. PIERPOINT AS A WHOLE HOGGER.

to place Warrington on the list with the other progres- sive towns of the country, and once more show that the workingmen had as much consideration for their own homes as the men in other districts who had already revolted against Tory rule. (Cheers.) Several members of the audience then asked questions which were answered by Mr. Howes. Councillor MAGINN proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman and speakers, and said the large and interested gathering was a proof that political spirit had improved in Warrington. The Government were doubted, mistrusted and despised even by their own electors. In Mr. Crosfleld they had a man who would do all in his power to represent the workingmen of Warrington honestly. The motion was carried with enthusiasm.

In responding the CHAIRMAN said the only way to win the election was for every workingman to do his best in his sphere of life to try and spread the principles they as Liberals were fighting for and to do his utmost to secure the return of Mr. Crosfield. When the election came, he hoped they would not let Warrington stand disgraced by having a retrogressive member, but that the town would take its stand among the progressives and add the mystic letters M.P. to Mr.Crosfleld's name. (Cheers.) Mr. CROSFIELD also acknowledged Cue compliment,and said they had had a splendid meeting. It was most creditable that such a grand gathering had assembled on such a beautiful evening, and it was very encouraging to those who for the last twenty years had tried to keep the Liberal end up, when things were not looking quite as cheerful as they were now, and when they had not the good chance which they now had of bringing War- rington into line with the most advanced and progressive towns in the country. lie devoutly hoped they would not make any mistake next time, but that they would do what they had set themselves oat to do. (Caeers).

LIBERAL OPEN-AIR MEETINGS.

MR. A H. GROSFIELD ON QUESTIONS

OF THE DAY.

His Attitude Towards Labour.

STIRRING ADDRESSES BY MR. JOSEPH HOWES.

Further evidence of Liberal activity in Warringtdn has beer. forthmning this week. In order to keep the political questions of the day before the minds of the workers, two splendid open-air meetings have been held. The audiences ltave been very large and enthusiastic, and the addresses by Mr. A. H. Crosfiejd and Mr. Joseph Howes have been listened to with deep interest. The meetings mark a new venture in local Liberalism, and should serve a useful purpose in educating the constituency. On Tuesday evening there was an encouraging gathering at Bridge Foot, and on the following evening a large crowd assembled on the open space opposite St. Paul's Church. Perhaps the most interesting matter alluded to, from a local stand- point, was the attitude of the Labour Representation Committee,and a question by a member of the audience drew from Mr. Crosfield an explanation of his position. Hr. Grosfield's remarks are fully reported below.

===== 0002601_19040723_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
TUESDAY'S MEETING AT BRIDGE FOOT.

The Issues on which the Election will

be Fought.

Tuesday evening's meeting at Bridge-foot was at the outset presided over by Alderman Pierpoint. Later,the Rev. Herbert Dunnico took the chair and delivered an address in which he alluded to the Labour party's posi- tion. There were also present on the platform :—Mossrs. A. H. Croattold, Joseph Howes, C. Jackson Holmes, and Councillor Magian. Excellent arrangements had been made by Mr. J. Clayton (Liberal agent), and his energetic assistants, Messrs. Frank Plumpton and J. Howes, jun. Alderman Pierpoint briefly called upon Mr. Crosfield to address the meeting.

SPEECH BY MR. A. H. CROSTIELD. CHINESE LABOUR. HOUSING. TRADES UNIONISM.

===== 0002601_19040723_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. HOWE 3 then addressed the meeting. They could not occupy their time much better on a pleasant summer evening,he said, than in meeting together in the open air and considering the way in which the present Government had mismanaged our national affairs.(Hear, hear.) There was never a time more than the present when me' of all shades of opinion should bring their common sense to bear upon the questions of the day and take a firm stand. Great changes-had taken place since the Tory Government came into power eight years ago. Look at the condition of the country then and now. They came into power when the national exchequer was full to overflowing and now, after only eight years, it was empty and the bottom knocked out. (Laughter.) When the Tories had any doles tp give they went along to the Dukes, but when they had taxes to put on they came to the labourers. Therefore any man who voted for a Tory candidate was practically kissing the foot that had kicked him—and would kick him again. (Cheer.) We did not hear much about old age pensions now. (Laughter.) But as Liberals they must keep that question alive, and bring about the day when out of the imperial exchequer provision should be made for dis- abled workers after a certain period. (Hear, hear.) There was a great deal of poverty in the country they knew only too well. Why was it? Because of Government mismanage- ment and that was due to the apathy of the people. Home legislation had been scandalously neglected by the present Government. No one was satisfied. The Education Act had caused chaos and confusion. We wanted to link the a.b.c. class with the university. (Hear, hear.) We wanted education and not creed. (Hear, hear.) Then there was the licensing question. The very fact that the Government dare not have it dis- cussed proved that it ought never to have been intro- duced. It was a bill to compensate the worst part of "the trade "—the least deserving part of " the trade." It was proposed to throw huge sums of money, into the laps of the rich brewers. Every man who was a tenant in a tied house would be a victim under the bill and the rich brewer would get the plum. The bill provided that out of national funds hngh sums of money should be given to the brewers. He supposed that statement would be contested and it would be pointed out that the money was not to come out of national , funds but out of the brewers own pockets. It was proposed to tax licences, and the Government had as much right to tax a licence as it had to tax any other form of property in the country. If it was right I to tax the people's tea it was right to tax the brewers' monopoly. But when the Government 'took a tax it became national property and belonged to the nation, and the Government proposed to tax licences and give the taxes back as compensation. Then why did they not give them the tea tax back as compensation ? (Laugh- ter and cheers.) Why tax the licences at all? Merely to throw money to the brewers. Even a section of the Trade—the tied house holders—were revolting against the 1311 because it took from them to give to the already rich brewer. The Government was going to make a monopoly of the trade. They knew they could not depend upon the members of trades unions for support because they had made it impossible for Mr. Shackleton's Bill to be introduced. (Hear,hear-) They knew they could not depend upon the poor tax- payer because they had pulled down the purchasing power by taxing the necessaries of life; and they were trying to make friends with the brewers in order that they would use their influence on the polling day. (Hear. hear.) Mr. Howes proceeded to refer to the landing of Chinese labourers in South Africa. some or whom, he said, escaped and were arrested. 'nes, were now to grow wealthy on a shilling a day. (Laughter.) We did not fight for the glory of the country but for the ' degradation of its working classes. We did not fight to find fields for the workers, hut to load into the pockets of a gang of alien foreigners, heaps of money which they never ought to possess. Better for us, that gold and diamonds had never been found in South Africa. Radicalism, continued the speaker, did not teach them to pull anybody down, but it demanded the right to lift a lot of people up. They wanted to put the lever of better legislation under the lower strata of society and give them a lift. For that purpose they came to such a town as this and said : "Surely when all the country is revolting against Tory doings, Warrington will not lag behind." (A voice: ."It will not"). They had a memberwho in political parlance was known as a whole-hogger. Every abomination perpetrated by the Government he seemed Inclined to support. He was going in all bad ways politically. While they had nothing to say against him personally, they had much to say against him as a politician. While they desired to say nothing against him individually they had lots to say against the methods in which he was supposed to repre- sent a great and important town like this. The Liberals brought before them a candadate with practical com- mon sevse, and commercial knowledge, a man who knew that the source of the country's wealth sprung from commerce, manufacturer and industrial activity, a man who knew that the source of our wealth could only be properly handled, preserved, and conserved by men who understood the requirements of the nation, who had strength of intellect and warmth of heart to attempt to solve the problems, a man who believed that the taxa- tion of ground values would be more economical, more financially sound and truly patriotic, than the taxing or bread and tea, and who stood in favour of the removal of the taxes from food and the providing of the revenue from the wealthy sources to which he (Mr. Howes) had referred, a man who sympathised with the toilers, who understood the requirements of the country and was not afraid of being called a "Little Englander" in his endeavour to improve the condition of England. A man of that kind they had no hesitation,in bringing before the electors. If the fight was to result in a victory it was necessary that they should take for their motto "Unity and enthusiasm." All the progressive forces must work together. Let them cease using hard words against any section or any individual of any section. Every man who was not a Tory, who ;was not retrograde and was not in favour of stagnation, every man who felt it was time that this iniquitous Government should be brought to an end, should put his shoulder to the next man's shoulder, and they should stand together in a solid rank. He hoped at the next election they would win such a victory that they would be able

===== 0002601_19040723_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
MEETING AT BEWSEY.

Mr. Crosfield and the Local Labour

Party.

A large crowd assembled on the open space opposite St. Paul's Church for Wednesday evening's meeting, Councillor James Evans presided, and was supported on the platform by Messrs. A. H. Crosdeld. Joseph Howes, C. E. Parker, C. J. Holmes, and W. Furness.

The CHAIRMAN said they were entering upon what he hoped would be a very successful campaign, resulting in a win at the election. (Hear, hear.) He did not think the present was a time when Liberals, either advanced!or otherwise, could afford division. (Hear, hear.) All broadminded Liberals should put their backs into the work inorder that they might place in power a Government that would carry out the wishes of the people. Again and again they ha l blamed Conservative Governments of the past for not doing things, but they might very fittingly charge the present Government with doing what they were never sent to power to do and what they never had the expressed opinion and authority of the nation for doing. The great principles, of Liberalism were by no means played out. They had not yet been fully played in." (Laughter.) What Liberals wanted to do, was to advance still further on the old lines. They wanted many measures of reform. From what source were they to obtain them? These re- forms were to come from the same kind of men and the same code of principles which had brought us the prosperity and success of the last fifty years.(Applause.) MR. CROSFIELD AND TORY FO_REIGN POLICY.

A REPLY TO VIE Bow:mail MEMBER.

Mr. A. ff. CELOSFIELD, who was enthusiastically re- ceived, said he had the honour of speaking to a very attentive and considerate audience on Tuesday evening, and he was afraid he occupied a good deal too much time. They had on the platform that night otherspeakers, who, he knew, were competent to instruct them much better than he, and he saw one or two gentlemen close to,that he would also like to see upon the platform and to hear upon some of the great questions that concerned them so deeply. On Tuesday evening he said that with their favour he would like to say a word or two on some of those foreign questions that concerned them indirectly almost as much as the home questions that they were specially interested in, and more particularly because the member for this borough had more or less

CHALLENGED THE WHOLE PROGRESSIVE PARTY in saying that at any rate the Conservative party was better qualified than they (the progessives) to deal with foreign questions. He would not trouble them by going very much into detail, but he wanted to recapitulate some of the wars in which we had squandered our resources during the long reign of Toryism which at last was coming to an end. (Hear, hear.) lie would like to ask them to consider what some of those wars had meant, and to say a word or two upon the whole question of war and how they as Liberals regarded it. Let him remind them of the expenditure of £10.000,000 or more which was incurred when we embarked on that very foolish campaign— •

THE CHITRAL EXPEDITION'.

===== 0002601_19040723_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
They knew that in the Resebery cabinet it was an open secret that there were unfortunate differences, but he was sure they must all consider it very significant that upon that question of all questions in that cabinet there was absolute unanimity as to the totally un- necessary character of the Chitral expedition. He re- membered well at the time it was compared to a man in a fortress in the old days when they were surrounded by moats, who built a bridge or a causeway halfway across his moat to meet the enemy, and if he talked for an hour he c )uld not give them a better illustration of the folly and the waste of that Chitral expedition. They must remember whose pockets those ten millions of expenditure had to come from. The industrial classes of the country who provided the revenue had to pay for those things. He would take next the

ABSURD ATTEMPT TO HUNT OUT VIE MAD IVIULLAEI. He would only say one word upon that—and it had often been said and was very true—that it was a question who were the maddest people, the Mullah or the poorly who ran after him. (Laughter.) Alluding to

THE EXPEDITION TO THIBET, Mr. Crosfield said he happened to know that even amongst the forward party of the military school of India—who very naturally, and if they would properly had plenty of fight in them—there was very little attempt to defend that wretched expedition, and he did not think there would be many of the Liberal party who were going to sanction or approve a waste of our resources on expeditions of that kind. He had left the greatest war in our times to the last, but if any of the audience had done him the honour to read the long letters in which he had endeavoured carefully to investi- gate the causes and the origin of THAT TERRIBLE CAMPAIGN IN SOUTH AFRICA, they would know that he held that there, quite as much as in those minor wars, judicious, thoughtful and wise statesmanship might easily have prevented if not all, at any rate an enormous amount of the waste of blood and treasure and the untold misery that the war had caused. They could depend upon it that the enormous expendi- ture in South Africa had affected them and their indus- tries at home. (Hear, hear.) It reacted upon British trade, and he thought it would make their hearts bleed if he told them what he had heard from eye witnesses of the Transvaal and the Orange Free State, where those unfortunate little nationalities representing a Popula- tion no larger than that of Salford were crushed out in that sad and terrible war. They would remember before that war came about, how they were told that they were going to fight the battle of the miners. [lad it been a miners' war, or was it, after all, a mine owners' war? (Hear, hear.) They could not have forgotton how they were told that they were going to develop a great white man's country. Now they had heard. and , they had all been stirred by the idea, that after the legagy that Wilberforce handed down and which had made England's name one of the very safeguards against slavery, they were going to have introduced in that country

===== 0002601_19040723_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
MOST SERVILE KIND OF CHISTESE LAMOUR.

He did not think thatany thoughtful workingman could be gulled or deluded over such a question, or fail to perceive that either the Chinese labourer was going to displace the white man or else he was going to crag down the conditions of white labour to something like those that obtained amongst the Chinese. He had been in America once or twice and he knew that of all the problems which that country had to deal with—and there were many difficult problems there—there was not one that was more serious and more ominous than what was called the negro question. (Hear, hear.) Black labour was introduced for the purpose of slavery from Africa into America. The blacks had been emancipated, but the mutual hatred and contempt which so largely existed between them and the white population of America was one of the most serious problems •that country had to deal with to-day. Were they quite sure that in years to come this reckless Govern- ment that stuck at nothing and considered nothing, would not have given them a very unfor- tunate legacy, if they had yellow and white labour mixed together under the conditions that obtainedat present in South Africa? He had ventured to speak strongly against the wars, little and big, which had been incurred since the Government came into power. They knew it was a,very old, old story—the inclination of the Conservative party to distract attention from reforms and progrees at home by a foreign policy of aggrandisement and excitement. He thought history was repeating itself in this long 'reign of the Tory party. He did not wish anyone pre- sent or anyone who might be kind enough to read his remarks to suppose that ha was or ever had been a peace at any price man, because he was not. (Hear, hear). There were WARS WITTCH WERE JUST AND NECESSARY. The most terrible war in modern times was that great conflict in America between north and south. He did not think people always realized what those four years of war cost America, but he might tell them that dur- ing that fearful civil war one million white men were killed on the field of battle, and that represented one half of the total number of lives lost during the Napoleon campaigns. He did not suppose anyone pre- sent would deny nevcrtheless that that great struggle bad to be fought out. That was a lust and necessary war. He had alluded to the great Napoleon. Every- body knew that Europe was bound to resist that man of marvellous ability and most unscrupulous character. He had to be beaten. That was another conflict that *aS just and necessary. Mr. Edmund Alfred Wallington, iron and steel merchant of Sutton, and formerly of Latchford, and a member of the Runcorn Board of Guardians and of the Rural District Council, who died at his residence, Sutton, Preston Brook, Cheshire, on May 2nd last, left estate valued at £4103 is. 3d. gross.

There was only one policy to deal with the Turk and that was the "bag and baggage" policy of Mr. Gladstone. (Cheers.) Referring to the South African war, he criticised the Government's mis- management of it, and said we knew now what we went to war for. (Hear, hear.) It was a war to make the rich, richer and the poor, poorer (Hear, hear). Discussing the attitude of the Tory party towards trades unionism,Mr. Howes said the whole history of trades unionism was a condemnation of the Tory party. There was a combination of em- ployers long before there was any combination of the workers, and men were imprisonel in the old days for combining together and doing what was perfectly legal today. There was now a deep laid conspiracy to de- prive the workers of the advantages of their toil and to further pull down the purchasing power of their wages and make their battle more difficult in the future than in the past. (Hear, hear). Unfortunately, the member for this Borough was a member of that con- spiracy, perhaps not knowingly. Mr. Pierpoint was a "whole hogger." No doubt he thought that the bad which was being done would result in good at some future time. (Laughter.) In referring to some af the problems confronting them, Mr. Howes referred to the necessity of dealing with the land question and the House of Lords. A Liberal Government in office was never in power. They had to depend upon the enthusiasm of the outside people to fight their battle successfully against the House of Lords, which existed to prop up the interests of the landlords and monopolists. Mr. Chamberlain asked the workers of this country to make sacrifices by giving Colonial preferences and enhancing the price of food. The workers of this country had passed through the days of protection and could speak from experience. When tariffs were high their wages were low. That was the state of things in every continental country to-day under protection. It was said that wages would increase under a system of preferential tariffs, but if the Government wanted to prove their sincerity in this matter let tnem pass a law compelling employers to pay the increased wages. A powerfully built navvy, from Belfast, was sentenced to twelve months' imprisonment at Scarborough ort Wednesday for a murderous assault upon Constable Temple. The Recorder highly complimented Emily* Watson, the wife of a painter, who pluckily went to the assistance of Temple after be had been felled by the prisoner and lay unconscious.

MUNICIPAL BYE-ELECTION. TO TIIE ELECTORS OF HOWLRY WARD. LADIES AND GENTLEMEN, At the request of a large number of ratepayers. I have consented to allow myself to be nominated as an inde- Pendent candidate at the b 3 erelection in this ward. I have always been of opinion that it was a mistake to let politils interfere with raunicipal affairs. and I welcomed the advent of the Non-trading Association, if only to kill the compact between the political parties. If they have done so they deserve the thanks of the town, irrespective of our opinions concerning their general policy, but personally,l think that they have justified their existence. They have scotched softe ridiculous schemes, they have exposed serious irregularities in connection with a slack Contract, and in many ways have enabled us to know more of the inner workings of ths Town Council than / formerly. As a large Ratepayer I have watched with interest the members of the Town Council embark upon the various costly public undertakings these last few years, and I consider that they have been guilty of lavish ex- penditure. The rrention of new Police Buildings at a cost of about £30,000 was a scandalous waste of public money, and if properly applied that sum was sufficient for that purpose and also the widening of Warrington Bridge. The continued high rates and their probable increase Is a serious matter to all concerned. They are detri— mental to the best interests of the town, a tax upon its trade and prosperity, and a cutting down of estimates as was recently done in Manchester would perhaps solve the question. I am 'not an advocate of false economy because I believe in efficiency, but when th 3 rates are increasing by leaps anti bounds it is time to economise some way. I am strongly opposed to increasing of salaries of already well paid officials of the Corporation by the ridiculous figure of £5O or £lOO at a time as was done recently, and respectfully suggest if any it creases are necessary that for a change the Council should commence at the other end of the social ladder. They have workmen in their employ who are not over paid. Some perform thankless duties for what is practically a bare living wage. I be- lieve in, and shall always support the payment of the standard rate of wags., both to workmen in the employ of the Corporation and also on contracts give,. by them. I am a strong supporter of the principle of Non-Trading. How can you expect purity or efficiency in municipal life if you allow members of the Council to use their positions in any way to gratify their personal gains The highest experts on municipal law have condemned the system. It is immoral ann leads to corruption and it should be declared illegal. In conclusion if you honour me with your confidence, I shall do my utmost for the ward of whieh I am a resi- dent. I would always oppose extravagance and am confident that you would never reget electing me to the honourable position as one of the representatives of Howley Ward in the Town Council. I am, Ladles and Gentlemen. 'Your obedient servant, THOS. CHANTLER, 92, Mersey Street; Warrington.

COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON.— MUNICIPAL BYE-ELFECTION, 1934. TO THE BURGHS MIS OF HOWLEY WARD. LADTES AND GENTLIMEN,— Having been unanimously selected by the Conserva- tive Ward Committee and at the request ol a large num- ber of personal friends.l have again consented to contest the Ward in the Conservative interest at the forthcoming Municipal Election, caused by the elevation of John Evans. Esq., one of your late and esteemed represen- tatives, to be an Alderman of the Borough. Being a large ratepayer and having resided amongst you for 36 years, I can justly claim to know tbe require- ments of this large and important ward. As a determined Advocate of economy I view with regret the ever-increasing Salaries paid to the exception- ally large staff of Officials which we at present employ. especially considering the high rates we have to pay and the heavy financial embarrassment with which the town is already overburdened. If elected. I should support a fair wage clause for the benefit of the workingmen in all Corporation Contracts. In conclusion, if on this occasion you houour me by electing me to the Town Council, I can assure you tlaat anything which may be brought forward for the better- ment of the Town and of the Howley Ward in particular shall have my meat cordial and emphatic support. Thanking you for past favours and once again trusting you will honour me by recording your votes on my behalf as early as convenient on Monday next. I beg to remain. Ladies and Gentlemen, Yours obediently. JACOB SANKEY. 21, Church-street, Warrington, July 18th, 1904.

Post. free 12 stamps —HoLDRorD'S, Medical Hall, The Clerk reported upon the county rate basis, and Cleckbeaton, Yorks. stated that he attended before the committee at Liver- poal with reference to the assessment of the parish. It was there resolved that the new gross assessment should be £12,790, being an increase of £460 on the valuation at present in their books. One item of interest was that the schools had been put tp on the valuation from £8 10s. to £l4. Previously the county had only paid on that £8 10s., but now the Rate Committee had raised the amount they would require to be paid on the extra figure. He might explain that the increase in the valuation of the district was generally put down at half what it was estimated would be the extra value in the next fouror five years. Thus,if it was thought that £4OO worth of property, etc., would be built in that time they would put the assessment up £2OO. Since the last valuation in 1899 the parish had increased in value by £2,000, so that although they could not expect the parish to go on at anything like that rate, the commit- tee had not put very much on them. What the Council had to Consider was whether they would appeal against the amount put on them if they thought it too much. He might add that Mr. Dodds was a member of the committee and complimented him upon the moderate assessment the committee had put on. It was decided to take no steps in the matter, the amount being con- sidered reasonabl,. SCHOOL MANAGERS. The Clerk reported that he had received a letter from Mr. Clarke, the clerk to the District Education Authority, stating that the names submitted by the Parish counAl had been approved, with the exception that Mr. J. Fulton Smith was appointed one of the school managers instead of Mr. Furness. The Chairman said he regretted Mr. Furness's name had been taken off the list. Mr. Higgs : We can do nothing in the matter. We only had to suggest names. The Clerk: That is so. This was all the business of public interest. SUICIDE WITH A BANKING ACCOUNT OF £400,000. Miss Bertha Dolbeer, who jumped from an upper window of the Waldorf-Astoria Hotel in New York, on July 8, leaves by her Will nearly £400,000 to her cousin, Miss Warren. Miss Warren, who had been deceased's companion for some years, was the only witness of the suicide. NO TIP FOR THE WAITER. The other day, after dining at a restaurant in the Strand, a gentleman from New York paid his bill, and was about to leave, When he was arrested by a signifi- cant cough from the attendant menial. "That does not include the waiter, sir," he said. " Ah," said the man after a moment's thought, " but I didn't eat the waiter !"
